# Blackwater Quay — The Complete Omnibus

## The Lantern That Did Not Cast Light

The face Banki wore served as a door, existing solely to hide what waited behind it. It presented a pleasant arrangement of features—sharp enough to demand caution from clerks and liars, unremarkable enough to be forgotten by dusk. Beneath the skin and the assumed humanity lay unyielding will, and beneath the will lay hunger. He craved structure, the hidden levers and pressure points by which a town could be made to kneel without ever knowing whose hand pressed against the back of its neck.

The Greybridge road unspooled before him, carrying the scent of wet dirt and old iron. The track snaked through the hills, worn deep into the earth by centuries of weary travellers dragging themselves from one miserable settlement to another. Every rut and pothole spoke of a profound, generational neglect. The mud whispered that no one maintained this route, and the silence reigning over the scrub promised that whatever happened out here stayed strictly between the participants and the crows.

It suited him perfectly.

## The Shape of a Lie

Survival, Banki had learned while still small enough for a crow to swallow, belonged to the creature capable of becoming unrecognizable.

He carried that lesson from the day the shining crusaders came to cleanse his birth-forest. They slaughtered the plainly dangerous fey first, moving with a righteous, terrifying efficiency, proving that good people were the most lethal creatures in existence. Once purity claimed a rigid definition, everything outside it became contamination. From the high canopy, the shape of their mercy had grown clear: strike the healer, blind the scout, break the leader, and wear whatever skin the world required to endure.

Now, he wore a human shape the way other men wore cloaks, with the absent ease of long practice. A man of middling height and unremarkable build trudged through the late afternoon chill, somewhere in his mid-thirties judging by the lines etched around his grey-green eyes. Those eyes carried the precise amount of weariness for a sellsword between engagements.

Beneath the flesh-weaving—beneath the bone and cartilage forced into mortal proportions—lay something ancient and jagged. Wings like stained glass stayed tightly folded against a spine sharp as a needle. A mind burned with a fire devoid of warmth. A pixie of the old deep woods, carrying in his hazelnut-sized skull an accumulation of tactical doctrine and raw, untamed magic that would have shattered a human archmage's sanity.

The world required none of that truth. The world required a lie walking a dirt road with nothing in his pockets worth stealing. Banki had always been extraordinarily generous with his lies.

He had walked for three hours when the emptiness of the road shattered.

He pushed his awareness outward, a silent cast of the mind slipping over the rolling hills. The ether rippled. Raw thoughts sparked in the gloom like candles lit in rapid sequence over the next rise.

One mind flared bright with pain and panic. Another ticked with sharp, self-satisfied control. A third remained cold, disciplined, holding itself utterly still.

A moment later, three more minds bloomed to the southeast. These were dimmer, moving fast through the winter brush, burdened by heavy effort.

Six minds in total. Three on the road, three in the scrub. The arrangement clicked into place with the precision of a tumblering lock. A broken wagon on the road ahead, a guard controlling the scene, and accomplices using the distraction to slip away with the cargo.

Banki's lips remained still. His stride held its rhythmic pace. His grey-green eyes swept the empty path ahead with the bored attention of a traveller possessed of entirely innocent intentions. Behind the mask, the ancient thing began calculating the grim arithmetic of the ambush.

## Cold Wind and Bad Intent

The sky above Greybridge hung heavy and bruised. Instead of stepping out onto the open road, Banki sank into the winter grass and scrub, letting his awareness stretch thin.

Fresh tracks bit deep into the brush, betraying multiple heavy-footed runners. A broken axle lay ahead, the wood shattered by a blunt strike rather than the wear of travel. A phantom restraint tightened and vanished under Banki's left shoulder, an old physical memory answering the threat of an orchestrated trap.

The wagon canted at a sick angle across the dirt, its left rear wheel snapped clean from the axle. The splintered ends shone pale and raw, the colour of exposed bone. Three figures stood around the wreckage in a crude morality play: the beaten driver, the authoritative guard, and the trapped traveller.

The guard wore a faded blue tabard bearing the Greybridge crest, the silver thread darkened to the shade of dirty dishwater.

Sixty feet separated Banki from the scene. He stood in the scrub brush, enveloped in a cloak woven with marrow-runes and bone-dust by long-dead craftsmen. The enchanted fabric drank the wind and the fading light, turning him into a shadow snagged on a thorn. The cost of wearing it gnawed at his core temperature, leaving frost in his veins, but the concealment was absolute.

He sank deeper into the minds ahead. The driver's panic spiked sharp as glass underfoot, a chaotic mess of agony and terror. The guard's thoughts ticked beside it in rigid clockwork, struggling to project outrage while managing the scene. The cloaked traveller remained a locked door with a pulse, trained enough to cage their fear, calculating the odds of survival.

Beyond the road, the three burdened minds thrashed through the brush. The carrier, the watcher, the fist.

Someone had built this stage by hand. They had positioned the wagon to block the road, cast the driver as a prop, and cornered the traveller as an unexpected complication. While the victims stared at the distraction, the real thieves vanished with the prize.

Safe and useless options presented themselves. Banki could simply walk away. But six minds offered six veins of leverage, and extracting the marrow from this scheme meant holding power in Greybridge before he even crossed its gates.

The choice crystallized. He would involve himself. He would take control.

The three thieves angled toward a bend in the road ahead. The guard's thoughts flickered toward the same bend, waiting for a signal.

Banki could reach the bend first. He melted into the brush.

## The Choir of Frightened Minds

Magic required no theatrical flourishes, no grand gestures to alert the prey. Banki adjusted his grip on his walking stick, weaving the spell into the mundane movement. His mental reach sharpened, cutting cleanly through the distance.

No one stirred. The guard maintained his bluster. The driver wept. The traveller stood frozen in disciplined terror. None of them sensed the invisible hand turning the pages of their minds.

Banki held his position, sifting the currents.

The guard's surface thoughts flowed rich and frantic: *...keep him talking... blame sticks if I keep the crowd framed... where are they... don't let the cloaked one bolt... Captain wants the goods quiet...*

Captain. A title carrying the weight of institutional command. The word "goods" echoed with bureaucratic neutrality—inventory, not plunder. The ambush operated on greased, systemic machinery.

The driver's mind offered only the pure, blinding noise of suffering: *...I told them I didn't have it—gods, my jaw—why would they hit me—just let me go—please don't take the wagon...*

Banki cataloged the driver's pain and set it aside. Collateral damage. No operational value.

The traveller's mind proved far more intricate: *...prints too fresh... three of them... don't look at the break—look at the guard's hands... if I move now, I'm next... where's my focus—stay calm...*

This one read the scene through brutal experience. The traveller recognized the fresh break and the wrongness of the guard's stance. A competent, disciplined survivor. Unshepherded, she might panic and ruin the board. Guided, she could become a crucial fulcrum.

Banki threaded a thought outward, piercing the distance like a needle passing through a cornea. The message had to be simple to survive the initial shock of mental intrusion.

*I am anchoring your mind. You can fight without restraint. The guard is stalling for partners and goods. Three sets of fresh prints in the brush. I will take the flank; you hold the center.*

The traveller's mind convulsed in a silent cascade of shock. Terror jagged upward, followed by a plunging chasm of confusion, before an ironclad discipline slammed into place. Banki read the jagged edges of her memories: she had been surprised before, and she knew the first second of hesitation brought death. Her hand twitched beneath her cloak and stilled. A breath caught, stopped, and returned under strict control.

*...not guard... help... okay... breathe... don't look for the source... just stand still and don't die...*

Banki allowed himself a flicker of professional appreciation.

He sent a final, brief directive. *When violence erupts at the bend, take the guard. Seize the narrative publicly. Make the driver your witness.*

A single, tight pulse of understanding echoed back. A compressed acknowledgment from a survivor ready to exploit a sudden advantage.

Banki withdrew the connection and slipped forward. He moved through the winter scrub like dark water through a cracked dam, slow and unyielding. Forty feet of brush stretched ahead. He timed his steps to the rhythmic sweeps of the guard's attention, stealing forward in the four-second intervals when the man's gaze swung toward the treeline.

Drop by drop, step by step, Banki closed the distance. A hundred and eighty pounds of lethal fey intelligence glided through the peripheral vision of the world, entirely unremarked.

The bend matched the image plucked from the guard's thoughts. A shallow cut in the terrain offered a natural blind spot. A low spine of rock, grey and furred with ancient lichen, provided waist-high cover. Between the rock and the thorns stretched twenty feet of open road—a kill lane. Anyone rounding the bend would stand completely exposed, unable to retreat without turning their back on the waiting executioner.

Banki settled behind the stone, pressing his illusory palms flat against the cold granite. He sifted through his vast repertoire of violence, selecting a working that others might consider excessive, but which he considered merely thorough.

His heartbeat slowed to a steady rhythm. The minds approached, heavy and confident.

Boots crunched on the packed dirt. A cold smile touched his lips.

## The Kill Lane

They rounded the bend carrying the swagger of men who owned the earth beneath their boots.

Three of them. The leader, built taut as a bowstring in patched leather, held a shortbow with the casual readiness of a lifelong killer. The second man loomed broad and thick. The third struggled behind, bearing the stolen weight.

Banki let them enter the lane. The kill required a cage.

He reached into the deep marrow of his magic, bypassing the slow chants of mortal sorcerers. He opened a vent to the deep earth, calling upon ancient roots and the void that slept beneath the bedrock. The magic drew heavily on his vitality, leaving a coppery taste in his mouth, but he welcomed the drain.

The ground erupted in a terrifying silence.

No explosive thunder warned the thieves. The magic manifested organically, the way rot blooms on a corpse or tree roots silently split a gravestone. Slick, writhing stalks of pure void burst from the packed dirt, reeking of ozone, stagnant water, and crushed bone. They were thick as a man's thigh, thrashing blindly through the dust.

The bowman met the void first. Three stalks coiled around his waist with the slow, crushing inevitability of a starved serpent. His shortbow clattered to the dirt. His jaw unhinged, but the unnatural pressure collapsed his thorax before his lungs could push out a scream. A wet, whistling wheeze escaped his lips, trapped and desperate. The stalks constricted further, and his ribs gave way with a sickening, wet crunch.

The carrier went down instantly. The stolen cargo spilled across the dirt as the shadowy vines seized his legs, dragging his face hard across the gravel.

The broad man proved tougher. Catching only a glancing blow to his shins, he stumbled but powered through with sheer mass. He staggered out of the thickest cluster of thrashing void-stalks, bloodied but upright, raising his cudgel to search for a target hidden behind the stone.

Banki rose from behind the lichen-covered rock. He gathered the ambient magic, twisting it into a curse of compressed force and bone-dust—a death-hex refined into a screaming bolt of pale light.

He aimed at the bowman, though the man was already dying, and released the hex.

The bolt crossed the lane faster than breath, striking the bowman's chest. The concussive impact shattered the remaining structure of the man's sternum, extinguishing his fading consciousness in an instant.

Down by the wagon, the traveller struck.

She timed her move flawlessly. Her voice rose, piercing the chaos with absolute, ringing certainty.

"He did this! The guard—he set this up!"

The words hit the scene like stones breaking a stagnant pond. The beaten driver flinched, his eyes wide as he stared at the guard, suddenly understanding the shape of his own ruin. The guard whipped his head toward the traveller, his mask of authority crumbling into raw, calculating panic.

Banki tasted the guard's spiraling thoughts: *...how did they—who told—control this, CONTROL this—Captain's going to—*

"Lies! You're with them!" the guard shouted, stepping backward. But the accusation carried no weight. The driver now looked at him with the terrifying clarity of a betrayed man.

The guard reached for his belt, his fingers frantic for a signal whistle.

Banki drew a second screaming hex. He leveled his hand at the broad thief, who had turned toward the sound of the shouting. The bolt of concussive force slammed into the large man's shoulder, tearing the armour at the seams and painting the cold air in red mist. The cudgel hit the ground. The broad man dropped to one knee, his aggression evaporating into a singular, crystalline realization of his own mortality.

The guard spun toward the bend, twelve feet away, running hard. His fingers closed on the whistle.

Banki shifted his aim. The third hex found the target that mattered.

## What the Guard Knew

The blast caught the guard perfectly between the shoulder blades.

He was caught mid-stride, his body entirely committed to forward momentum. The concussive impact struck with the wet crack of a thunderclap, violently accelerating the man's flight while destroying his ability to control it. The guard vaulted through the air, his limbs windmilling uselessly. The metal whistle slipped from his grasp, tumbling away into the deep scrub.

He hit the dirt face-first and skidded to a halt. Groaning, he dragged himself to his hands and knees, his mind a shrieking mess of denial and agony. *...magic—who—can't—Captain, Captain, CAPTAIN...*

Banki stepped clear of the rock.

He offered no grand pronouncements. The road simply yielded a man in a dark cloak, eyes cold as a frozen river, his right hand still smoking with the residue of a shattered hex.

"Stay down," Banki commanded, his tone dropping to a quiet, iron rasp.

The guard collapsed into the dirt.

Banki crossed the distance with unhurried, measured steps, coming to stand over the broken man.

"Captain Veller," he murmured.

The guard froze. Raw, animal terror eclipsed his pain.

*...how does he—no one knows—who is this—HOW DOES HE KNOW—*

"The south culvert," Banki continued, his voice perfectly level. "The Greybridge tollhouse ledger."

Each phrase hammered into the guard's fragile composure, detonating the walls protecting his secrets. As the defenses shattered, the information flooded into Banki's mind. He drank the torrent of memories: the names of the garrison conspirators, the smuggling routes, the ferry marks, the bribes, and the shallow graves hidden beneath freshly poured foundations. He archived every detail in the dark vaults of his mind, savoring the acquisition.

The guard sobbed uncontrollably into the dust. Banki offered the man no pity.

Four minutes later, the traveller walked up the bend.

The void-stalks had dissolved back into the earth, leaving only scored dirt and the broken bodies of the thieves. Banki had bound all three survivors with coarse rope.

The traveller stopped ten feet away and lowered her hood, revealing a woman with sharp, exhausted features.

She carried no obvious weapons, but Banki's arcane sight traced a faint thread of warding magic woven into her cloak, a quickening charm around her boots, and a vicious, single-use evocation trapped within a silver ring on her right hand. She stood perfectly balanced, evaluating him with the cold precision of a veteran who knew precisely how quickly men could turn into monsters.

*You anchored me,* she projected no words, but he read the shape of the thought forming in her mind as she spoke aloud. "You anchored me in my head. You provided an invisible safety net."

"Yes," Banki replied, declining to elaborate.

She studied the bound men, the cratered earth, and the lingering scent of ozone and blood.

"What are you?" she asked.

"A traveller." The lie slipped out flawlessly. "One who happened to possess the right skills at the necessary moment."

Her eyes narrowed in rapid calculation. She weighed his usefulness against his lethality. "My name is Sable. And I owe you a debt I did not ask to incur."

"Then we understand each other perfectly. Unsolicited debts form the foundation of ancient pacts and every truly useful relationship." Banki smiled, calculating the exact value of the interest he intended to collect.

Behind them, the bowman finally stopped breathing. Neither of them turned to look.

## The Captain's Shadow

Banki wasted no time on interrogation. Questions allowed a prisoner the luxury of resistance and choice. Instead, he placed a hand flat against the guard's chest, pushed past the surface panic, and broke the locks on the lower vaults of the man's memory.

The guard shattered without making a sound.

The sensation of the breaking mind—the sudden, breathless freefall of a consciousness losing its anchor—dragged Banki violently backward in time.

*The sterile, blinding light of a Thessalan Consortium holding cell burned his retinas. He was strapped to a chair forged of pure cold iron, the heavy bands biting deep into his wrists and throat. For a creature of the deep woods, cold iron was not merely a restraint; it was a localized cremation. The metal seared his flesh on contact, filling the air with the sickening sweet stench of roasting fey-blood, ensuring he was entirely consumed by the physical agony when the interrogator, a faceless silhouette standing behind the glare, engaged the mnemonic extractor. The wipe didn't gently erase memories; it flayed them out, layer by agonizing layer, leaving only smooth, dead ash in its wake. But Banki had refused to surrender the architecture. As the machine tore his surface identity to shreds and the iron ate through his skin, he had driven the most crucial secrets—the names, the routes, the true shape of the Consortium's rot—deep into his own autonomic nervous system. He forced himself to encode the data into the rhythm of his breathing, into the desperate stutter of his failing heart, hiding the knowledge beneath the threshold of conscious thought while the extractor sterilized his higher mind. Surviving the mind-wipe meant burning alive while becoming an empty house that secretly remembered how to rebuild itself.*

The memory receded, leaving only the cold wind of the Greybridge road and the broken guard trembling beneath his hand.

The ledger spilled open in Banki's mind. Drop points, cargo schedules, the fear of Captain Veller, the shadow of the patron commanding them all. Banki sifted through the trauma, extracting every usable ounce of leverage, leaving the man hollowed out and weeping around his gag. The guard would remember the intrusion only as a terrible, crushing weather inside his own skull.

Sable stood by, her posture completely undisturbed by the violence. Banki caught the peripheral echoes of her memories—a brutal education on the streets of Blackwater Quay—and realized this violation of the mind registered as simply another tool of survival in her estimation.

"He will remember enough to be terrified," Sable said, her words barely carrying over the wind.

"Good."

"Terrified men run."

Banki released his grip on the guard, his grey-green eyes gleaming with the deep, cold light of the forest. "Let him run. A running man always leads you back to whoever holds his leash."


"Then we give him somewhere useful to run."

The network arranged itself behind Banki's eyes, a sprawling map of complicity and commerce.

The tollhouse served as the hinge. Through it moved money, protection, smuggled goods, and people who had become cargo once enough respectable officials learned to spell profit with clean hands. Shellbreak Cove took payments through a chapel pew. Greybridge merchants washed coin through suppliers existing only on paper. Veller's confidence stemmed from a source much higher than a town guard captain should be able to reach.

"The driver goes free," Banki instructed. "He remains a tool, a mere participant."

"And these three?" Sable gestured toward the bound men.

"Assets. For now."

She fixed a hard stare on the guard. "He knows you violated his mind."

"Yes."

"That matters."

Banki turned towards her, the road wind moving through the tall grass between them. "It matters operationally. Shame makes poor witnesses."

"That is exactly my point."

The guard's awareness made him inconvenient.

They carried that knowledge into Greybridge by dusk.

The town had been built by optimists and maintained by pessimists. Wide streets narrowed under decades of neglect. Stonework that once promised civic pride wore thick coats of mildew and soot. At the East Gate, Veller's lookout leaned against a wall, his attention drifting toward the taverns.

Banki altered his height, shifting his hair color and the drape of his cloak before crossing the threshold. Sable entered later from another angle. The lookout’s gaze slid past them both, a mercy born of incompetence rather than illusion.

They convened in a square where the fountain had died years ago.

"The tollhouse," Banki murmured. "North side of the bridge. The real ledger sits beneath the counter. Mechanical lock. Minor ward."

"Tonight?"

"After midnight."

Sable adjusted her collar. "Then make it quiet."

A thin smile touched Banki's lips. Silence remained his sharpest tool.

At the Broken Wheel, they took adjacent rooms. Banki sifted through the guard's stolen memories while Sable mapped exits, rooflines, and the innkeeper's level of curiosity. Various routes wove through the night, but one path led to a cabinet Veller believed safe. The corrupt often confused secrecy with ownership.

At midnight, Banki slipped out through the window.

The tollhouse keeper snored softly over empty bottles. The roof possessed a gap no adult would consider and no pixie could resist. Banki flowed inside in his true form, wings folded tight against his spine, and descended to the cabinet beneath the counter.

A standard ward sealed the ordinary iron lock.

He left the door closed. Pressing his mind against the internal mechanisms, he forced the bolt aside, grinding a sliver of space to pass through without waking the arcane alarm. The effort drew a sharp, metallic sting across his temples, a small price for the intrusion.

Inside waited papers, wax seals, and the leather-bound journal that mattered.

The ledger.

He absorbed every page, taking nothing but knowledge.

Veller's routes ran deeper than the guard had realized. Three adjacent smuggling networks. Monthly payments. Coded protection. The patron's mark repeated often enough to form a signature, allowing Banki to compare it against his sprawling mental archive.

Lord Castellan Aldren Prost.

The regional governor's chief of staff.

The man possessed the authority to redirect investigations, reassign guards, and force the law to defend the crimes it had been written to punish.

Banki closed the ledger, re-engaged the bolt with a telekinetic nudge, and left the ward sleeping undisturbed.

Back at the inn, he sat on the edge of the bed in total darkness until the mental map ceased its chaotic shifting and solidified into actionable leverage. A single knock rapped through the plaster wall.

"Alive?" Sable asked.

"Productive."

He considered layering a lie over his exhaustion, but chose utility instead. "No alarms. No bodies."

After a long pause, she tapped the wall once. Acknowledged.

Through the thin plaster, Sable's breathing remained too even, too shallow for sleep. He knew she kept a knife under her pillow, hoarding questions she did not yet trust herself to ask. A rustle of blankets betrayed her shifting weight. Alive did not mean unhurt, and the absence of bodies did not mean no one had been damaged. He filed her guarded vigilance for later.

Banki lay back, studying the shadowed ceiling. He possessed the patron's name, Veller's routes, and the first useful shape of a regional machine.

At dawn, he would begin deciding which parts of the machine to buy, which parts to break, and which parts to wear.

### The Checkpoint

The approach to Eastgate-on-Mere smelled of river muck, woodsmoke, and the sharp copper tang of too many people crowded behind stone walls. Ahead, the checkpoint gate loomed as a choke point of lanterns and spiked iron. Carts and wagons bottlenecked under the arches, oxen lowing in the fog.

Banki sat beside Kreel on the driver’s bench. The wagon shifted, its suspension groaning under the weight of covered cages and sealed jars. Inside those tarps rested a fortune in illegal and regulated blink-fauna.

Kreel eased the reins. The wagon slowed to a crawl. "They're thorough tonight," the driver muttered, his gaze fixed on the gate guards pulling apart a peddler’s cart.

The hum of the mind-space—the telepathic web Banki always kept spun out around him—caught the thoughts of the gate guards before the wagon even rolled to a halt. He let his focus widen, catching the psychic runoff of the men in tabards.

*Do it by the book. Log the wagon. Don’t spook the horses.* That was the sergeant, leaning against a halberd, irritated and tired.

*Permits. Manifests. Look for mismatches. If it’s specimen work, verify seals. Try to find a reason to squeeze.* That was the clerk, a pale man with ink-stained fingers and greedy eyes.

Not opportunistic bandits. Bureaucrats. Far more dangerous.

"We play this straight," Banki murmured. "Mara."

The girl slid off the back of the wagon before it fully stopped, smoothing the front of her simple merchant-apprentice tunic. She carried a leather folio thick enough to stop an arrow.

The clerk—Hask, according to the bronze tag on his chest—stepped up, raising a hand. "Hold. Manifest and cargo declaration. We’re doing full inspections for contraband. Illithid scare on the Low Wharf means command wants every crate cracked."

Hask’s mind flickered with the anticipation of finding something wrong. A broken seal. An unlisted cage. A reason to demand thirty gold to look the other way.

Mara didn't give him a single inch of breathing room. She stepped forward, opening the folio, her voice a relentless, practiced drone. "Of course. Here are our receipts from Greyfen Cross, Sablemarch Gate, and Harrowmile. Here’s our stamped tariff class, and here’s our running ledger with totals. You’ll note the third page details our sealed specimen transport bonds, pre-cleared by the Creature Gate Yard."

She began laying documents onto Hask's portable desk. Neat stacks. Stamped wax. Perfectly tallied ledgers. It was a paperwall, thick and utterly impenetrable.

Hask blinked. His mind faltered, the greed replaced by the sheer, crushing boredom of auditing a flawlessly kept ledger. He pawed at a page. "Mm. I still need to see the back of the wagon. The cages—"

Banki shifted on the bench. He let the cowl of his cloak fall back just enough to reveal calm, unblinking eyes. He didn't push into Hask's mind. He just let the weight of his presence settle over the clerk like a collapsing roof. "We’re happy to comply," Banki said, his voice flat, reasonable, and cold as a grave. "Just make sure you’re writing the inspection log correctly. I’ll wait."

Hask swallowed. The psychic pressure of a predator mimicking a merchant made the hair on his arms stand up.

The gate sergeant, already losing patience with the line of cursing teamsters behind them, stepped over. "Hask. Either inspect them or clear them. We’re not holding the gate all night for your hobby."

Hask’s irritation flared, but compliance won out. He slammed a heavy iron stamp down on Mara's top sheet. "Fine. Tariff only. Twelve gold."

Banki tossed a small leather purse down. It clinked on the wood. Hask scooped it up, not meeting his eyes, and waved them through.

The wagon rolled under the portcullis. Eastgate-on-Mere opened up before them, a sprawling, chaotic maze of opportunity and eyes.

### The Hearth & Ledger

They passed the official caravan yard—too much scrutiny, too many wardens—and ignored the cheap stable yards smelling of rot and desperate men. Banki directed Kreel toward Blackbarrow, a district of merchants who preferred quiet wealth over loud displays.

The Hearth & Ledger sat off a main thoroughfare, boasting a clean common room, private tables, and an iron-gated yard in the back. It asked for coin, not stories.

Banki paid the innkeeper nine gold for three nights, securing three rooms on the second floor. One for himself, one for Mara and Elowen, one for Sable. He carried the heavy lockbox and the Bag of Holding up the stairs himself.

Once the door of his room clicked shut, he summoned the crew.

They stood in the center of the clean, wood-paneled room. Mara clutched her folio to her chest. Elowen looked exhausted, her clothes still carrying the faint musk of the cages. Kreel stood by the door, arms crossed. Sable lounged against the window frame, a shadow pretending to be a man, his eyes flicking toward the street below.

"We have a three-day window," Banki said. His voice didn't rise above a murmur, but it commanded the room completely. "We have secure wagons, rested animals, and beds. This is a frontier city. We have money to spend, and we will do it quietly."

He met each of their eyes in turn.

"House rules. No one drinks. Loose tongues get us killed or audited, and I prefer the former. Mara, Elowen, you do not wander the streets alone. You go out in pairs, or you take Sable. Sable, you rotate between yard checks and inn presence. Nothing moves near the wagons without you knowing about it."

Sable gave a single, tight nod. "Understood."

Banki patted the heavy leather satchel slung across his chest. "The Bag of Holding stays closed in public. We are boring merchants resting between hauls. You buy cold-weather gear, spare tack, and whatever provisions we need for the Border Wilds. You act like the money we have is the only money we possess."

Mara and Elowen both exhaled, the tension bleeding out of their shoulders. The strict boundaries were a comfort. Structure meant safety.

"Get some sleep," Banki said. "I have a delivery to make."

### The Creature Gate Yard

The air in the Creature Gate Yard tasted of ozone and terrified animals. It was a sprawling, screened-in labyrinth of holding pens, appraisal tables, and heavily warded vaults.

Renn’s screened bay sat in the back, shadowed by a canvas awning.

Banki stepped in, dropping the heavy reinforced crate onto the scarred wooden table. He kept his telepathy tuned tight, a pinpoint beam focused entirely on Renn. No wandering into the yard-hands' minds.

Renn stood across the table, a jeweler's loupe strapped to his right eye, his hands stained with alchemical ink. He didn't look at the paperwork. He watched Banki's face.

*He wants the numbers fast,* Renn's surface thoughts whispered into Banki's mind. *He wants to know if this lane is real.*

No deception. Just a broker doing his job.

Renn leaned in slightly. "Break the seal. Let’s see what the mud dragged in."

Banki cracked the wax seal on the crate and folded back the padded lid. Violet light spilled out, flickering and erratic.

Sixty jars sat nested in the velvet-lined dividers. Each contained a piece of the blink-fauna ecosystem. Skitters, mostly—multi-legged things that vibrated in the fluid, occasionally popping out of existence only to reappear an inch to the left with a soft *thwip* against the glass.

Renn sucked in a breath. He pulled a sheet of heavy vellum from a drawer. "The Academy Blink Pricing Grid. A thirty-day standing lane. Read it. Then I’ll translate what the words actually mean."

Banki scanned the document. It was written by a clerk obsessed with conditions. Standard skitter jars fetched a hundred and ten gold. Premium certified, one thirty-five. Higher fauna—hoppers and newts—pushed past two hundred. Deductions for poor water quality, compromised seals, or stressed specimens.

"They're rewarding volume," Renn said, tapping a line near the bottom. "Consistency bonus. If you deliver fifty or more jars in a single submission, they add ten percent to the total payout. That is what being a supplier means."

Renn began pulling the jars out, holding them up to the lantern light, checking the seals, tapping the glass.

"Fifty-eight skitter jars," Renn tallied, his voice a low hum of calculation. "Two hopper jars. Two riftleaf newts."

"That's sixty-two," Banki noted.

"The newts are part of the sixty," Renn corrected smoothly. "We classify them under Tier Two, but they count toward the volume bonus. Let's do the conservative math."

Renn dipped a quill and scratched numbers onto a slate. The scratching filled the quiet bay.

"Fifty-eight skitters at one-ten. Six thousand, three hundred and eighty. Two hoppers at two-twenty. Four-forty. Two newts at one-eighty. Three-sixty. Base subtotal is seven thousand, one hundred and eighty gold."

Renn drew a sharp line under the number.

"Consistency bonus. Ten percent. Adds seven hundred and eighteen." He circled the final figure. "Projected Academy payout: seven thousand, eight hundred and ninety-eight gold. And that’s if none of these grade out as premium."

Nearly eight thousand gold. A staggering sum. It was the kind of money that bought armies, ruined noble houses, or in Banki’s case, paved the road to a much larger game.

Renn looked up. His mind hummed with steady, professional satisfaction. *He's going to like this. This is steady.*

"They’ll remit in two days through the usual channel," Renn said, wiping the quill clean. "I can hold it as CGY credit, or I can front it from my float and settle with the Academy later. Your call."

"Hold it as credit," Banki said. A man carrying eight thousand gold in a bag was a target. A man with an ironclad line of credit at the CGY was a power broker. "I have a few specialized purchases to make."

### The Veilbound Needle

M. Vell did not keep a storefront. He kept a cellar beneath a disused chandlery on the edge of the dockward. The air down here tasted of burned copper, dried sage, and old blood.

Banki sat in a high-backed leather chair. The desk between him and Vell was entirely bare, save for a single brass candlestick.

Vell was a thin, gray man. He looked like an under-clerk who had spent entirely too much time in the archives. But his mind was an iron fortress. Banki’s telepathic sweeps slid off Vell’s consciousness like water off oiled glass. The man was heavily warded.

"I need an infiltrator," Banki said. "A scout. Something that doesn't set off common wards and doesn't leave footprints. Something a few tiers above an imp."

Vell nodded slowly. He steepled his fingers. "No preference means you’re pragmatic. Scout and combat means you’re ambitious. Willing to pay means we can actually talk."

Vell unlocked a drawer and pulled out a thin, black-bound ledger and a sheet of vellum. He began sketching a binding circle on the paper with a charcoal pencil.

"I’ll give you the truth," Vell murmured, not looking up from his sketch. "'A few tiers above an imp' is Tier Two if you want it to stay quiet, and Tier Three if you want power with catastrophic consequences. Tier Two is the sweet spot. Real utility. Real bite. Not so loud that every interested party in the city turns their head when you use it."

"What are my options?"

"Three archetypes," Vell said. "The Ashwing Censor. Flies, sniffs out abjuration magic, can shatter minor wards. The Saltglass Sentinel. A bodyguard. Tough, camouflaged, takes a hit meant for you."

Vell paused, his pencil hovering over the paper.

"And then there is the Veilbound Needle."

"Tell me about the Needle."

Vell looked up. His eyes were entirely black in the dim light. "Infiltrator. Silent kill assist. Courier. It has a short-range blink. It can render itself invisible to mundane and minor magical sight. It possesses a venom strike that can paralyze rather than kill, if you require a breathing captive. Its stealth is... exceptional."

It was perfect. A ghost to pair with Banki’s telepathy. A surgical tool to remove problems before they ever realized they were in danger.

"I want the Needle," Banki said.

Vell nodded. He wrote a series of numbers beside the circle. "The ritual fee is three thousand, five hundred gold. Materials—circle external inks, specific salts, a lens-crystal, and anchor nails—will run one thousand, five hundred. And I require a collateral of one thousand gold, deposited into a sealed escrow sigil. It returns to you in thirty days, provided you do not breach the pact."

Six thousand gold to begin.

Banki reached into his coat, retrieving a heavy pouch of cut gems and minted platinum. He set it on the desk. It landed with a dense, heavy thud. "Timeline."

Vell didn't touch the gold. He looked at it respectfully. "Two nights to prepare the circle and acquire the specific entity through my channels. The binding ritual occurs on the third night. It will take three hours."

Vell slid a fresh sheet of paper forward. "You will sign three non-negotiable clauses. First, the Discretion Clause. No public manifestation in populated areas unless it is a matter of immediate life or death. Second, the Obedience Clause. It follows commands, but it will refuse a suicidal order or an order that breaks this contract. Third, the Release Clause. If the bond is breached, the entity returns to my circle, not loose into the world."

Vell dipped a pen in an inkwell that smelled sharply of iron.

"I am adding a fourth clause, free of charge. The Loyalty Clause. Betrayal of the master triggers binding pain and forced recall. It tightens your control."

Banki took the pen. The ink felt thick, almost warm against the parchment. He signed the name of his current cover, binding his intent to the paper.

"Three nights," Banki said, standing up.

"Three nights," Vell agreed, finally resting a hand on the pouch of gold. "Try not to die before then. The entity will be quite disappointed."

Banki walked back out into the fog of the city. He had the money. He had the crew. And in three nights, he would have an invisible killer bound to his will. 

Eastgate-on-Mere didn't know it yet, but the city belonged to him.


## The Signature That Bled

Cities pretend they are stone, but Blackwater Quay had been built on ledgers before pilings, resting entirely on the patient fiction that ink could force hunger to behave.

Banki claimed the Yard, though the ink had yet to dry. Payment remained the vulgar part. Coin crossed tables, changed purses, embarrassed honest men, and comforted dishonest ones. Ownership required witness, and witness required paper. Paper demanded the Municipal Transfer Registry, and the Registry demanded a clerk whose conscience had survived just long enough to become inconvenient.

His name was Caspel Rooke.

He was forty-six, narrow in the shoulders, careful with his cuffs, and possessed of the exhausted moral rigidity common to minor bureaucrats lacking the power to become grandly corrupt. His record of bribery amounted to two minor infractions: a widow's dock permit moved up three weeks for a feverish son, and a fishmonger's tax arrears marked paid because the man wept and Caspel remembered childhood hunger. These sins comforted him, merciful enough to be renamed.

Tonight, mercy rendered him useless.

"The Butcher's Alley compound is under investigation," Caspel announced. His registry lamp smoked between them, painting his face with yellow edges. "No transfers until the governor's office clears the title."

Sable stood by the door. Mara anchored herself near the file shelves, one hand resting on the folio that contained every legal path available should the man concede. Twenty-three paths existed. None survived a refusal entered before midnight.

An ink stain marked the clerk's second knuckle. A mild tremor shook his hand, bearing a paper-knife scar on the thumb. A pulse fluttered visibly at his throat, beating far too fast for the cool room. He was a man frightened by his visitors, not the request.

"Who ordered the hold?" Banki asked.

"The record states provisional review."

"That was not my question."

Caspel swallowed hard. "Lord Prost's office."

A wire in the machine hummed to life.

Bribery would fail. Fear had already purchased the clerk's loyalty. Threats against his family would be inefficient and vulgar.

He needed a signature.

The room narrowed.

Banki drew upon a three-fold binding spell, waking the magic without a single gesture. Ozone sharpened the stagnant air. The pressure dropped just enough for Mara to touch her ear and for the lamp flame to bow towards Banki, gravity yielding to power. The spirit's refraction split the clerk's outline into three overlapping phantoms: the body that sat at the desk, the terror struggling to flee it, and the obedience Banki prepared to stitch between them.

Caspel gasped, his hands spasming against the desk. The mind always recognized violation before it understood the method.

"Please," the clerk whispered.

Banki despised the word for how it sought to recruit witnesses.

Sable remained perfectly still. Mara's jaw tightened.

Banki opened his inner sight to the crystalline architecture of the man's soul.

Caspel's mind arranged itself in sharp, defined lines: ledger-loyalty, fear of Prost, pride in the registry, love of procedure, and a deep resentment of nobles who used clerks as shields. Beneath it all lay a buried wish, small and sour, to sign one document in his life because it was correct rather than because someone above him had leaned on his spine.

Creating obedience from nothing demanded too much brute force. Instead, Banki found the buried wish and pulled.

The spell translated the man's conscience into tactile sensations against Banki's mind: dry vellum, brittle twine, damp wool, a brass key worn smooth by generations of hands. Banki threaded compulsion through the textures with the precision of a surgeon suturing a nerve, ignoring the sharp copper ache building behind his own eyes.

Sign because the hold is improper.

Caspel whimpered.

Sign because procedure requires a clean title when the investigation concerns the former owner, not the property.

His fingers twitched toward the pen.

Sign because Lord Prost has already corrupted the process, and you have always hated corrupted process.

The clerk wept with quiet professionalism, treating his tears as another document to be filed in duplicate.

Banki pushed one layer deeper.

Erasing Caspel Rooke would be wasteful. Instead, Banki removed the man's ability to feel the wound. He altered the memory's angle, reweighted causality, softening the place where terror would have become resistance and hardening the resentment into courage. When the weaving finished, Caspel would remember being afraid, then angry, then correct. He would believe the signature had been his first honest act in years.

The pen scratched across the parchment, filling in a name, two dates, and securing three wax seals.

The Yard opened under Banki's hand.

When the final impression cooled in the wax, Caspel sat back with the empty dignity of a man who had been robbed, thanking his robber for balancing the account.

"The transfer is lawful," he whispered.

"Yes," Banki agreed softly. "It is."

Outside, rain ticked against the registry windows like fingernails on coffin wood.

Mara took the folio, her voice clipped. "We have title."

Sable remained by the door, her hand resting near her blade. "That was a person."

Banki studied the signed document, the ink still wet enough to shine.

"Tonight," he said, "he was both."

Sable's expression remained frozen, but the air in the room grew colder than the rain outside.

By dawn, wagons would move. Locks would change. Work crews would enter a compound that had belonged to a dead criminal yesterday and belonged to Banki Sovereign now, simply because ink, witness, and a violated clerk had taught the city what to call it.

## A Knife Wakes Sharp

Twelve days later, Banki woke sharp, his mind cutting straight through the morning fog.

The Yard hummed in the pre-dawn dark. Wagons loomed under dew-heavy tarps while perimeter lanterns burned low. The compound smelled of damp wood, oiled leather, and the sour metallic tang of creatures despising their cages.

He stood at the window of the temporary office—a converted loft above the primary stable block—and studied the shifting shadows below. Blackwater Quay required constant calibration. Ownership remained a hypothesis tested daily by thieves, rivals, and the city watch, who were often just thieves with better tailoring.

"The perimeter is secure," Mara stated from the desk behind him, refusing to look up from her ledger. She never lifted her eyes when a number could be bullied into compliance instead. "Sable's people caught two spotters from the Tidegate gang last night. Both were discouraged."

"Lethally?"

"Structurally. One broke an ankle falling off a roof he wasn't on. The other decided he'd rather work on a whaling ship and is currently halfway to the deep water."

Banki turned from the window. "Efficient."

"I am an accountant, Banki. Waste offends me." She closed the ledger. "We face a problem with the magistrate’s office. The noise complaints regarding the loading bays have escalated."

"Who?"

"Magistrate Vane. He is ambitious, underpaid, and clearly signaling that he wants a retainer."

"Put him on retainer."

"It sets a precedent."

"Precedents are just prices by another name," Banki countered. "Pay him. Ensure the payment arrives in a manner suggesting we could easily take it back if he becomes inconvenient."

Mara nodded, scratching a note. "I will have Renn handle it. He possesses a gift for making bribes feel like threats."

"And the recruits?"

Sable entered the office, silent as smoke, carrying a sheaf of paper smelling of cheap ink and wet stone.

"Sorted," she announced, dropping the papers onto the desk. "Seventeen viable. Five useless. Three informants."

"For whom?" Banki asked.

"Two for the city watch. One for Lord Prost."

Banki’s eyes narrowed. The ambient temperature in the room dropped a few degrees.

"Prost," he murmured. "He is watching the Yard."

"He is watching the ink dry on Caspel's signature," Sable corrected. "He knows the transfer was forced. He doesn't know how, but he knows."

"Then we let him watch," Banki said. "Give the informant something to report. Let them see a legitimate business operating with aggressive efficiency. We give them no shadows to chase, only ledgers they cannot balance."

Sable crossed her arms. "And if they don't want ledgers? If they want blood?"

"Then we ensure it is their own."

Mara tapped her pen against the wood. "I have filed the operational structure with the guild. As far as the city is concerned, we operate as a logistics and procurement firm specializing in exotic goods. It offers a broad enough category to cover most of what we do, and remains vague enough to annoy anyone attempting to tax us."

"And the physical expansion?" Banki asked.

"The adjacent lots are ready for purchase," Mara replied. "But we require more leverage to keep the price down."

"I will acquire it," Banki said.

"By coercing another clerk?" Sable asked. Her voice remained flat, devoid of judgment, but the question hung in the air like a blade on a thread.

"By understanding what the current owners value more than property," Banki replied smoothly. "Sometimes that is money. Sometimes safety. Sometimes the illusion of choice."

He walked back to the window, staring out at the Yard as the pale light of dawn bled through the fog.

"We are building a machine," he murmured, more to himself than to them. "Every person in this city acts as a gear, a spring, a lever. Most of them do not realize it. Our job is not to break them. Our job is to place them in the correct position so the machine turns the way we require."

"And if a gear doesn't fit?" Sable asked.

Banki kept his gaze on the yard. "Then we file it down until it does."

Mara closed her second ledger with a sharp snap that echoed like a gunshot. "I will draw up the acquisition papers. We move on the lots tomorrow."

"Agreed," Banki said.

The machine turned. The gears slipped into place. Blackwater Quay had bared its throat to him, and he intended to swallow it whole.

## The Assessment

The morning wore on, the fog lifting to reveal the grim, purposeful activity of the Yard. Handlers moved beasts in reinforced cages, clerks tallied manifests with frantic precision, and guards walked the perimeter with hands resting comfortably on weapon hilts. It functioned as a symphony of controlled aggression, with Banki serving as its conductor.

He spent the next three hours in the lower holds, reviewing the latest acquisitions. A brace of shadow-stalkers from the deep woods prowled their enclosure, eyes glowing like embers in the gloom. A young wyvern hissed venom that sizzled against the stone floor, its wings bound tight against its sides. A pair of basalisk eggs rested in lead crates packed with enchanted ice.

These creatures represented value, danger, and above all, leverage.

He stood before the wyvern's cage, calculating the profit margin on its venom, when his awareness snagged on a thought entirely disconnected from the ambient hum of the Yard.

It felt sharp. Directed. Malignant.

*...just one slip... let the chain drop... make it look like an accident...*

Banki kept his head perfectly still, but his focus snapped to the source like a compass needle finding north.

A handler stood ten feet away, working the winch on the shadow-stalker cage. The man's face formed a mask of pure concentration, but his mind boiled with a chaotic swirl of resentment, greed, and a highly specific plan.

*...they paid half up front... the rest when the beast breaks loose... lots of confusion... I slip out the back...*

Banki recognized the pattern immediately. Sabotage. The clumsy, desperate variety that usually ended in significant collateral damage.

He turned slowly, locking his gaze onto the handler.

The man froze, the heavy chain slipping slightly in his grip. The shadow-stalker lunged inside its cage, hitting the iron bars with a heavy thud that rattled the moorings.

"Secure that chain," Banki commanded, his voice carrying effortlessly over the din of the hold.

The handler swallowed hard, his knuckles turning white against the metal. *...he knows... how does he know... act natural...*

"I said, secure it," Banki repeated, taking a measured step closer.

The handler fumbled with the locking pin, his hands trembling violently. He drove it home just as Banki reached him.

Banki remained silent. He let his presence push against the man's mind—just a fraction, just enough to let the handler feel the sheer, crushing weight of the intellect arrayed against him.

The man's knees buckled. His thoughts dissolved into raw panic.

"Who paid you?" Banki asked, his voice barely a whisper that nonetheless echoed like thunder inside the handler's skull.

"T-Tidegate," the man stammered, sweat beading on his forehead. "They... they said just let one loose. Cause a distraction."

"A distraction," Banki mused. "How pedestrian."

He reached out and tapped the man on the chest, a gesture so casual it was terrifying.

"You will go to the paymaster," Banki instructed. "You will collect your wages. Then you will leave this Yard and never return. If you show your face in Blackwater Quay again, I will not kill you. I will simply remove your ability to understand language."

The handler nodded frantically, stumbling backward before turning and fleeing towards the stairs.

Banki tracked his retreat, feeling the familiar, cold satisfaction of a threat neutralized. The machine remained fragile in its early stages, requiring constant maintenance.

He turned back to the shadow-stalker. The beast growled, a low, rumbling vibration vibrating deep in its chest. Banki met those glowing eyes, acknowledging the raw, hungry intelligence behind them.

"We understand each other," he murmured.

He turned and headed back up to the office, his mind already shifting to the next variable. The city overflowed with leverage, waiting to be found and used.

And Banki possessed endless patience.

## The Second Ring

The office sat quiet when he returned. Sable stood by the window, gazing out over the Yard.

"You handled the saboteur," she stated without turning.

"I removed a defective gear," Banki replied, taking his seat at the desk and pulling a fresh sheet of vellum toward him.

"You let him live."

"Dead men tell no tales, and they certainly do not spread fear," Banki noted, dipping a quill in ink. "He will run back to Tidegate. He will describe what happened. They will realize their clumsy disruptions are useless, forcing them to try something more sophisticated. Or, preferably, they will give up and pay tribute."

Sable turned, her expression unreadable. "You play a long game."

"The only game worth playing," Banki replied. "Short-term gains belong to thieves and politicians. We build an institution."

He began to write, his hand moving in precise, elegant strokes.

"What is that?" Sable asked, nodding toward the vellum.

"A formal invitation," Banki said. "To Magistrate Vane. It is time we had a face-to-face conversation about his retainer."

"In person? Here?"

"No. On neutral ground. The Whispering Lamp, in the Silk District. Public enough to make him feel safe, expensive enough to flatter his ego."

"I will arrange security," Sable offered.

"Do," Banki agreed. "And Sable?"

She paused at the threshold.

"Ensure the security is highly visible. I want him to know we can reach him, but I also want him to know we choose not to. Let him feel the restraint. It often proves more persuasive than violence."

"Understood," she replied, vanishing into the corridor.

Mara stepped back into the office, carrying a finalized stack of acquisition papers. Banki finished his letter, signed it with a flourish, and set it aside to dry.

"And if I had preferred leverage?" Banki asked, referencing their earlier conversation regarding the guild filing.

"Then you would have won one frightened clerk and lost a legal category," Mara said smoothly, organizing her files. "I won the category."

The shape of her strategy crystallized in his mind. She hadn't built a favor, a bribe, or a threat. She had constructed a public word the city now had to use when speaking about him: Containment.

Mara took the notice back and filed it before he could decide whether to be angry. By noon, Caspel's signature had become a thing clerks repeated carefully. No one said coerced, and no one said clean. They simply said filed, a word that in Blackwater Quay often proved more dangerous than the truth.

Sable returned to the doorway, her voice calm as a closing latch. "The recruits on probation are responding. Some are eager. Some are lying. I can sort the rot out early if you give me permission."

An old laboratory memory stirred when Banki noted the complex ward drawn across Mara's ledger. He chose not to dissect it. Sable's guarded mind, present and deliberately shielded, offered enough difficulty for one morning.

Then, a third mind brushed against the edges of his awareness.

It felt faint, distant—not in the office, but nearby, somewhere in the wagon lanes or the rigging racks. The thought carried a hooked edge that made Banki's attention snap toward it with predatory focus:

*...if the boss is distracted, the wagon plan is mine...*

Possession. Plan. Timing. This was not the idle daydream of a laborer fantasizing about advancement. It was the pointed intentionality of someone identifying an opportunity and preparing to seize it.

*...third pin from the left, swap with the scored one... when the harness fails it will look like a defect. Then the boss backs my design. Then it's mine...*

More sabotage.

Banki's face revealed nothing. His voice remained a quiet, controlled instrument. "Mara. Hold those items for ten minutes."

He rose from the desk, walking out of the office and descending back into the fog of the Yard.

## The Saboteur's Thoughts

He located the second saboteur near the rigging racks.

The man possessed an unremarkable build, cloaked in the shadows of the harnesses.

Banki’s witch-sight, anchored by the runes carved into his very bones, caught the unnatural glimmer immediately: a faint transmutation aura clung to the tool roll. It wasn't strong—a cheap, mass-produced charm-work stamped out with bone-dust, the kind purchased in any mid-sized market—but it remained deliberate. Tools that had been magically modified to perform beyond their apparent function.

The man crouched near a harness assembly, his hands moving with the quiet, focused competence of someone intending to finish before anyone thought to ask questions. His surface thoughts spun in a tight loop of concentration and schedule:

*...swap with the scored one, she'll never check before the trial run...*

Banki stopped fifteen feet away. He did not speak. He did not move. He simply stood in the damp air, letting his presence fill the space between them like water filling a glass—slowly, completely, displacing everything else.

The saboteur stiffened, sensing the sudden, crushing weight of Banki's attention before he even looked up. The muscles in the back of his neck contracted. His hands slowed, stopped, and retreated from the harness assembly with the careful withdrawal of a man realizing he touched something lethal.

His eyes came up, finding Banki's. In that half-second of contact, his surface thoughts erupted in sheer panic:

*...he's looking at me—don't think about it—DON'T THINK ABOUT IT—act normal—he can't know—just a routine check—DON'T THINK OF THE BOSS—*

It mirrored the mental equivalent of a man trying to hide by screaming his own location at the top of his lungs.

Banki let the silence stretch. Five seconds. Ten. The saboteur's thoughts spiraled into increasingly frantic loops of denial, each loop weaker than the last, eroding the fiction that everything remained normal. The man's composure stood like a sandcastle in a rising tide—technically intact, but clearly doomed.

"Show me the tools," Banki commanded softly. "The real ones."

The saboteur's hands trembled violently. His thoughts collapsed into a single, bright point of surrender: *He knows. He knows everything. He's standing right there and there's nowhere to run.*

Defeated, the man opened the tool roll.


Inside the satchel, nestled among the grease-stained wrenches and iron files of a legitimate yard-hand, lay a set of scored replacement harness pins.

A single fractured pin during Vexa’s morning trial run would have snapped the traces, ruined the animal, and shattered the yard's rising reputation in a spray of blood and public embarrassment.

"Who bought you?" Banki asked.

The saboteur shrank back against the stable wall, his silence breaking under the weight of Banki's unblinking stare. The man babbled the details. Two hundred gold. A cheap price for a man’s loyalty.

Banki weighed the broken iron in his palm, running his thumb over the jagged fissure. He calculated the variables, letting the cold, divine geometry of power balance the scales. The conclusion arrived crisp and inevitable.

Vexa’s trial ran without incident that afternoon. The handlers found the replacement pins because Banki ensured they stumbled upon them, leaving three would-be imitators to understand that treachery in the yard always left fingerprints.

"You will continue your shifts," Banki told the saboteur, his voice carrying the quiet finality of a closing tomb. "You will install the unmarred pins. By nightfall, you will detail every syllable of your arrangement to Sable—names, locations, and the precise coinage involved." Banki stepped closer, letting the man breathe in the scent of his own looming ruin. "Disobedience means I bypass your tongue and harvest the information straight from your mind. That extraction leaves the skull hollowed, rendering your remaining days short and agonizing."

He let the threat settle into the man’s bones.

"After that," Banki said, "you work for me. A man who understands sabotage understands the architecture of a system, and I require builders. The wage exceeds two hundred gold. The penalty for failure exceeds death."

The saboteur’s throat bobbed. Behind the man's wide eyes, Banki traced the frantic, flailing thoughts as they raced down every available avenue of escape, only to find Banki standing at the end of each one.

"Yes, sir," the man whispered.

"Good." Banki turned his back on him. "Tell Mara I require her in five minutes."

He strode toward the yard office, the coastal fog parting around him like a deferential servant. Behind him, the saboteur's hands shook violently as he began securing the heavy brass pins.

## Sable's Sorting

A growing enterprise eventually demanded a physical corpse: stone walls, holding pens, hidden expansion routes, and just enough visibility to pretend at legitimacy.

Mara had scouted the options, but Sable had walked the properties. Tidegate Yard offered service-quay access and adjacent parcels of soft mud perfect for quiet burials. Low Wharf stank of cheap frontage and uncontrolled danger. Slipstone screamed of unwanted attention. Ultimately, Banki chose the yard. He demanded dock leverage. He demanded room to expand. Most importantly, he needed a fortified stronghold for the operations legitimacy could not say aloud. Two floors, a reinforced basement, redundant exits. Sable built it expecting fire, betrayal, or siege.

Now, the cellar beneath the Gull & Gasket smelled of sea-salt, damp masonry, and cured fear.

Sable had arranged the architecture of the room to serve as an interrogation. Intake by the stairs stripped away arrogance. The bolted chairs sat close enough that a recruit’s ragged breath became physical pressure against her skin. The salted corner killed echoes. Every chalk line on the floor delivered a lesson before Sable ever opened her mouth.

Tonight was Rank Trials.

Banki lingered in the deep shadows by the stairwell. The air carried the faint, ozone tang of his active mind peeling back the thoughts of every soul she assessed. But he kept silent. The verdicts belonged to her. The room only functioned because the authority was undeniably hers.

Nell approached first. She carried the heavy shoulders of a dock-worker and the hollow, assessing eyes of a survivor.

"Because I excel at this." Nell squared her stance. "Being competent at the filth no one else wants to touch remains the only leverage a rat like me ever gets."

Sable held the silence for three heartbeats, letting the quiet stretch until it became a physical weight. She gave a sharp nod. "Survive the next test."

The others cycled through. Tams offered a clumsy fabrication about a surveillance route, the lie dropping into the heavy air like a rotting fish. Pike scraped by; his terror was visceral, but his answers stayed true under pressure. Orin navigated the interrogation cleanly, defining his limitations before they could become excuses.

The recruit designated S6 analyzed a theoretical field disaster by calmly explaining how he would have neutralized the threat during the planning phase, dirtying the extraction route long before entry.

Sable allowed a fraction of an inch of movement in her left eyebrow. She withheld verbal praise—satisfaction made desperate people sloppy, and sloppy people became liabilities. But she allowed the posture of the room to shift, letting the survivors bask in the harsh geometry of her approval.

When the final initiate retreated up the stairs, Sable extended the clipboard toward the shadowed corner. "Nell and Orin promote. Pike extends. Tams failed. Relocate and scrub his memory. S6 gets formal logistics integration."

Banki emerged, his features catching the flickering lantern light. "Execute it."

He moved toward the stairs, his footsteps completely silent on the damp stone.

"The structure is hardening," Sable said, pausing by the bottom step.

Banki didn't turn around. "That is the design."

"I know." She crossed her arms. "It keeps me here."

He ascended, leaving her alone in the quiet cellar. The chalk marks held their severe angles.

Usually, endless tasks padded the hollow hours of the night. Sable treated sleep as a strict, transactional tax, paying the body two hours at a time, never surrendering to actual rest. Tonight, however, the ledgers blurred into meaningless ink. The silence in the cellar expanded, leaving too much room for ghosts.

Kargrim’s face swam into her mind. The stonemason had needed forty-seven gold for his daughter’s Bridge Warden apprenticeship. Banki had peeled open the man’s private, fragile hope with the casual grace of a butcher snapping a rib. Kargrim's face had crumpled the exact second he realized his innermost thoughts were laid bare. It wasn't the visceral panic of physical torture. It was the crushing intimacy of realizing your mind was made of glass, and a stranger held the hammer.

The texture of that exact violation remained burned into her own memory.

She splashed a heavy measure of Edric’s cheap, caustic whisky into a chipped glass and threw it back, welcoming the burn. At twenty-three, her former handler had taught her that same lesson. She had long ago scrubbed his true name from her active recall, burying it under thick mental scar tissue. But the sensory echoes remained. The copper stink of a ruined safehouse. His voice framing atrocities as simple necessities. The sickening realization that his rigid ethics were merely a mask painted with clean hands.

The conditioning had started small. Missions that pushed right up against her moral boundaries. Assassinations framed as theoretical logic-puzzles. Targets issued with carefully curated dossiers, allowing her to pretend that ignorance equated to discipline.

Then the atmosphere had shifted. The missions grew fouler, framed by an insidious pressure demanding she accept the butchery as righteous. If she named it evil, she would have had to fight him.

Instead, she had gone out a third-story window and spent two years running. She burned through aliases, cities, and safehouses until her entire existence narrowed into a desperate map of emergency exits.

Then Banki had found her.

Banki possessed his own brand of monstrosity, but he never dressed it in virtue. He demanded power and admitted it. He sought absolute order and declared it. If his dark pacts and blood-arithmetic required slaughter, he named it slaughter. He didn't demand gratitude for the blade at her throat.

That absolute, unvarnished honesty made him legible, and in Sable’s brutal world, a legible threat was infinitely easier to navigate than righteous mercy armed with a lock and key.

She rinsed the glass in the iron basin and stared into the lightless corner of the cellar. Kargrim would be home by now, his memory neatly excised. His daughter would still hunger for the apprenticeship, the gold still out of reach. Neither would remember the monstrous intrusion, while the woman in the cellar drank alone, hyper-aware of her own fragile walls.

"I chose this," she whispered to the damp stone.

The darkness absorbed the words without judgment.

She climbed the stairs.

Banki waited on the landing. He stood off to the side, one shoulder resting against the rotting doorframe. His grey-green eyes were impossibly still. With the instinctual certainty she applied to blade-balance, Sable recognized the atmospheric shift of him sensing her distress bleeding up through the floorboards.

His focus drifted toward her mind, an invisible, crushing weight. The air pressure in the hallway dropped.

Then, he stopped.

The restraint was microscopic. Anyone else would have perceived nothing. To Sable, it struck with the staggering impact of a drawn weapon being slowly, deliberately sheathed.

"You are agitated," Banki said.

"That is an observation." Sable kept her voice level. "Not permission to enter."

"I am aware."

He didn't push. He didn't invade.

That deliberate restraint terrified her more than the violation would have. Absolute power was simple when it took what it wanted. Power that possessed the capability to take, yet chose to wait, created space for desire. Fear kept the emergency exits clear. Desire rearranged the floor plan while you weren't looking.

Her gaze locked onto the sharp line of his jaw before tracking his hands, a weakness she despised. She loathed that his ruthless competence radiated a magnetic heat. Most of all, she hated that the physical pull had taken root long before any genuine trust, giving the trust a dangerous bargaining chip.

"You protect your assets," she said, stepping closer.

"I do."

"Learn this now." She pointed a finger at his chest, stopping an inch from the fabric of his coat. "Protection does not equal partnership. I stand beside you. I do not stand under you, nor do I cower behind your wards in some gilded cage you label safety."

Banki’s gaze sharpened into a dark, predatory interest. "And if I forget that arrangement?"

"I vanish."

The words cost her everything to say, but they rang with absolute truth.

Banki studied her, his silence altering the very architecture of the hallway. Slowly, he inclined his head.

"Then I will remember."

It wasn't an apology. It was a binding treaty carved in stone, signed to prevent a war before the first shot was fired.

Sable brushed past him, the heat of his arm grazing her shoulder. She wanted to lean into that heat.

She trusted that impulse least of all.

## The Dead-Drop Chapel

The uneasy truce of their partnership held through the damp weeks that followed, right up until Blackwater Quay began swallowing their people. It wasn't a bureaucratic error or a simple desertion. Bodies were vanishing in the mist between streetlamps.

By the time Banki arrived at the district in person, the shadows had teeth. Two probationary scouts were missing. A third had wandered back with his memories surgically excised. Their dead-letter drops had been breached and carefully resealed. The local hierarchy—the Thieves Guild, the Harbour Magistracy, and the Drowned Choir—had all shifted into a crooked, aggressive alignment.

Banki approached the Dead-Drop Chapel. It stood on a wedge of rotting land between two overflowing canals, devoid of bells or congregants, offering nothing but stubborn mold.

Inside, the heavy scent of old wax and wet stone clogged the air. The short nave boasted a few warped pews, leading to a cracked altar and a confessional booth that leaned awkwardly, like a spy straining to eavesdrop.

Sable waited in the shadows near the back, her hood drawn up. Her posture appeared relaxed, every loose tendon telegraphing a deadly, coiled lie. She offered no greeting, merely jutting her chin toward the altar.

Resting on the cracked stone sat a small blackwood reliquary—their dead-drop box.

Banki expanded his psychic awareness into the room. Two distinct anomalies flared in his inner eye. First, a sickly, ozone residue clung to the box, the unmistakable stench of factory-stamped ritual divination. Second, a consciousness huddled near the confessional, flattened so meticulously that a lesser telepath would have mistaken the discipline for an empty room.

The Drowned Choir had left a spy.

Sable moved closer, keeping her voice under the ambient drone of the outside rain. "Branch is blown. Two missing. One returned grinning and hollowed out like a gourd." She scraped back a lantern shutter, casting a narrow beam of light onto the wooden support beam.

Carved into the grain was a coin, split perfectly by an unblinking eye.

"We didn't carve it," Sable murmured. "We didn't catch the hand that did. They wanted us to find it." She locked eyes with him, a builder defending her cracking foundation, demanding to know if he would draw his blade or flee. "Escalate, or keep it quiet? I can start shattering kneecaps until someone sings."

Banki peeled back the layers of the flattened mind hiding in the confessional. A faint, aquatic hymn drifted from the suppressed consciousness, confirming the Choir's involvement.

A cold, humorless smile touched Banki's lips. "Quiet. We start with the rat in the box."

Sable’s hand dropped to her hip—not drawing steel, but signaling readiness in their private hand-cant. Her body shifted from idle to lethal in a fraction of a second.

The spy's terror bloomed against Banki's psychic snare. The trapped woman realized she was hunted.

Banki moved with unnatural speed. He bypassed the confessional entirely, crossing to the chapel's heavy ironbound door. A single, echoing clunk sealed the only exit.

Simultaneously, Sable claimed the center aisle, boxing the spy in. Banki wove a psychic anchor around Sable's mind, shielding her from the Choir’s harmonic static so she could fight without impairment. Sable didn't rely on magic; she used the pure geometry of the room, positioning herself to control the killing angles.

"Emerge," Banki called out. His tone remained perfectly conversational, the genial patience of a predator offering its prey a momentary illusion of dignity before the slaughter.

Silence stretched tight.

Banki drove his awareness deeper, bypassing the spy's trained mental blocks like a master thief picking a cheap lock. The pressure cracked her defenses. Her name was Halvine. She had held her breathing and her thoughts perfectly still for three agonizing days. She carried a concealed blade, a mirrored signaling token, and a glass vial strapped to her wrist containing a volatile alchemical blood-quickener.

"I will say this only once." Banki addressed the wooden slats. "The vial on your wrist. If it is an enhancement draught, drink it or don't. I care very little. If it is a suicide poison, leave it stoppered. I require the contents of your skull, and I require the skull to be breathing."

The heavy velvet curtain of the confessional twitched, shifting a fraction of an inch. A single, dilated eye appeared in the gap, sharp with the frantic arithmetic of survival.

"You are the telepath," Halvine whispered, her voice layered with the hollow calm of standard interrogation-resistance training. "The one they warned us about."

"They warned you?" Banki asked.

"They mentioned a reader in the district. They failed to mention..." The professional facade cracked, leaking raw awe. "...they failed to mention you were this strong."

Banki filed the compliment away with a craftsman’s grim satisfaction. "The vial."

Halvine stepped through the curtain. She moved with deliberate, agonizing slowness, possessing the lean, coiled musculature of a professional runner. The blade remained hidden. Slowly, she raised her wrist, displaying the glass vial like an offering.

"Blood-quickener," Halvine said. "Thirty seconds of heightened reflexes. It isn't poison."

"Place it on the pew."

She complied, the glass clinking softly against the rotting wood.

Banki invaded her mind. He didn't tear her memories down to the studs; he performed a surgical, devastating scan, mapping the entire local branch of the Choir's spy network while leaving Halvine perfectly intact.

Once he had the map, Banki offered Sable a minute nod.

Sable closed the distance with terrifying, economical violence. Two steps, a blurred pivot, and a precise strike to the base of the spy’s skull. She caught Halvine before the woman’s knees hit the stone, lowering the unconscious body without a whisper of sound.

"Sack her?" Sable asked, already reaching for the bindings.

"Later. The dead-drop requires our attention."

Banki approached the altar and inspected the blackwood box. The seal had been fractured and expertly re-stamped. Inside, Sable's encoded logistics report lay untouched—indecipherable to the Choir, but entirely compromised as a secure channel.

Banki traced the wood grain, a new design locking into place. "The chapel is burned," he murmured. "But we maintain it. It becomes a honeypot. Every parchment we leave here will be meticulously curated to feed the Choir exactly the lies we want them to swallow. We steer them toward our phantoms and away from our true assets."

Sable’s lips curled into a rare, genuine smile, appreciating the weaponization of their own failure. "I draft the fictions?"

"You draft. I refine. We dictate their next move."

They slipped out of the chapel at midnight, leaving the confessional empty and the shadows undisturbed. The freezing fog swallowed them whole, indifferent to the empire they were quietly building in the dark.

## The Drowned Counting Room

The intelligence extracted from Halvine led them directly to the Drowned Counting Room, a half-submerged vault beneath the Gull & Gasket that Banki instantly admired for its ugly, utilitarian arrogance.

The Choir had anchored three harmonic nodes into the bedrock of Blackwater Quay, using a network of resonant crystals and a central tuning apparatus to passively manipulate the district. The subtle magic smoothed over unpleasant memories, nudged allegiances, and forced the locals to sing the Choir’s praises without realizing they had been coerced.

Banki didn't intend to preach against it. He intended to tear the mechanism out by its roots.

Sable vetoed his initial strategy, criticizing it for being too elegant and drawing too much attention. "If three nodes shatter simultaneously, they know a predator is hunting them," she argued. "Immediate panic is expensive. Gradual decay is useful."

So they layered the sabotage in mud and mundane misfortune. Sable orchestrated a symphony of petty disasters. A bribed ferryman raised hell over a stolen lantern. Street urchins swapped canal markers in the fog, forgetting the silver in their pockets seconds later. A mule cart snapped an axle right in the path of a Choir patrol. The clumsiness provided perfect cover.

Sable drowned the northern sluice node first, plunging three acolytes into the dark without triggering a single alarm. Banki took the Counting Room.

He slipped through the icy canal water, wrapping himself in a veil of shadow and bent light, while Sable silently eliminated the watcher on the towpath.
