The conduit remained optional and separately activatable, bounded strictly to this agreement. He drew the infernal force by express recorded invocation, accepting the burden frame cleanly. 

Kestrel’s mind merged with his, unimpressed by the sheer size of the creatures. Null-Fang hovered at Banki's shoulder, the spectral weapon's haft-mouth tasting the ambient pressure.

*Bite the weak commands first,* Null-Fang murmured into his thoughts.

Kestrel countered instantly. *Make them harder to fool.*

The Sovereign Thread solidified into a heavy cable of Pact-magic. Banki drove the power through all fourteen beasts, measuring their survival instincts against his mandated adaptations. He pushed the working to a critical threshold, fracturing the arcane structure. He did not clone the beasts. Cloning invited stagnation. Instead, he forced a volatile biological triplication, splintering fourteen originals into forty-two distinct entities—each bearing slight, necessary variances to guarantee survival.

He channeled the blistering resonance of his pact, substituting delicate spellcraft with marrow-burning runes of command. *No later infernal interest in acts, effects, derivative forms, or strategic consequences arising from lawful use of transferred force shall be presumed or implied.* He hammered the legal stricture directly into their neural pathways. Asmodeus would claim no tithe on the blood these beasts spilled. The violence belonged exclusively to Sablehook. Any later infernal interest must arise only from separately recorded express terms, stated narrowly and without spillover beyond the exact grant.

The psychic backlash tasted like ash and copper, searing his nerves as he forced the discipline deep into their biology.

Hit. Halt. Wheel. Redirect. Capture without crush. Crush only when a siege left mercy no room to breathe. Kestrel’s overriding command anchored the chaotic minds. False voices dissolved into meaningless noise.

Banki pushed the heavy cable deeper. The handlers flooded the pens with drums, shouting, illithid domination grammar, and false pain. The young beasts held their ground, ignoring the noise. Only when the Crown kill-halt settled into their instincts did Sable stiffen beside him.

"You should have asked before binding me into the command structure," she said, her voice dropping.

"Lives are at immediate risk."

"That is an explanation, Banki. It is not permission."

Banki met her gaze. He held the chaotic, roaring magic at a standstill. The magic burned through his stamina, agonizing in its stasis, while forty-two enormous bodies trembled on the edge of receiving doctrine. He forced the working to wait until she made her choice.

Her anger shifted, cooling into something sharp and metallic. "Operational wartime command. Not ownership. Not a leash."

"Command in crisis. Refusal rights in misuse."

"Put that in the herd."

He wove her terms into the working. The second batch assimilated the magic perfectly. Rage cooled into tool-use. The beasts learned to navigate around allies, surrendered captives, and bonded handlers.

During the final drill, a handler dropped a command-token and froze. A young beast lunged. Hesta’s hand shot toward the stop-cord, but the beast simply scooped up the token and the handler, setting them both behind the safety rail before turning its massive horn toward the training dummy.

Darric slumped onto a crate, exhaling a long, shuddering breath.

Captain Thorn-Rusk peeled off a leather glove and rested two fingers against the glass. "Authenticated. Brutal, but authenticated."

Mara drew two distinct boxes on her slate. The first listed the Caravan Line, public and regulated. The second box carried Banki's and Sable's personal seals, marking the black-tier assets.

"If these war-beasts step onto public stone," Mara said, tapping the second box, "the city will remember the hoofprints. We will face songs, panic, and courts. I cannot let the law leash what it cannot survive seeing."

"Accepted," Banki agreed.

She snapped the slate shut. "You just handed me a future bill, Banki. I fully intend to collect."

Deep beneath the breakfast hall, forty-two massive creatures dreamed in displacement haze, twitching as they rehearsed the motions of war.

## First Contact

The Guttered Lantern served as neutral ground, its peace maintained by the massive, heavily armed proprietor, Darva Wellspring. Banki arrived early to meet Serren Vale, a broker handling the upcoming auction cycle. He chose the tavern precisely because its neutrality offered a shield against unwanted eyes. The air smelled of sour ale, wet wool, and the sharp brine of the harbor. 

Banki claimed a corner table, placing his back against the damp plaster wall. He kept the shrieking, coiled pressure of his pact-magic tight in his palm, ready to unleash a localized devastation if the meeting turned foul. He let his telepathic awareness ripple outward, touching the ambient minds in the tavern like a spider resting upon a wide web. The lower rumor channels hummed with alignment-drift. The city was actively deciding whether to attach itself to his Hall, keep its head down, or gamble on the old power webs.

The tavern door swung open. A man stepped through, blazing in Banki’s telepathic field like a bonfire in a cellar.

Aldric Thorne carried a heavy, imposing certainty. Though dressed in common wool and leather, he moved with the rigid bearing of a man accustomed to plate armor. His eyes swept the room, seeking burdens to shoulder rather than exits to exploit. A woman followed him—Maren. The bard carried no instrument today. She wore unassuming, functional clothes and a warm, strategic smile that invited conversation while betraying nothing. 

They claimed a table three paces away.

Banki’s telepathic sense brushed Aldric’s mind, immediately encountering a blinding wall of righteous purpose. Fragments of thought bled through the paladin's shields: memories of the Choir, a dozen restored minds, the divine authority of a sun god, and a lingering, unresolved shadow. Aldric was searching for the root of the city’s corruption, circling the edges of Banki’s own syndicate without yet knowing its name. 

Banki swallowed a spoonful of fish stew, carefully digesting the thoughts of a man who intended to burn his empire to the ground. 

Then Maren turned her head.

She locked eyes with Banki, possessing the unnerving focus of a predator who had spotted a mismatched shadow in the undergrowth. Banki arranged his features into an expression of mild, polite curiosity, offering the harmless smile of a businessman evaluating a stranger's attention.

Maren returned a brilliant, professional smile and stood, closing the distance between their tables with natural grace. "Excuse me," she murmured, resting her fingertips on the edge of his table. "I’m sorry to intrude. I’m new to the city, and you appear wonderfully established. Would you mind if I asked a few questions about the district? I’m struggling to get my bearings."

Through the telepathic web, Banki absorbed Aldric’s sudden spike of concern. The paladin's hand twitched toward a sword he wasn't wearing, a reflexive preparation for violence seamlessly woven into his unconscious habits. 

Banki broadened his harmless smile, burying his lethal readiness beneath layers of practiced charm. "Of course. Please, have a seat. I’m Banki. I run a modest salvage business down by the Wharfside. And the fish stew here is exceptional, if you haven't ordered yet."

Maren sat. She possessed a musician’s flawless timing, asking gentle, meandering questions that left wide silences for Banki to fill. Banki fed her true facts meticulously arranged to form elaborate deceptions. He applied the Improvement Doctrine to the conversation, treating every probe as a material negotiation statement. When she opened a scope for broad inquiry, he offered responsive refinements, narrowing the venue of the conversation until she found herself boxed into mundane logistics. 

He spoke of Sablehook, routine auctions, and difficult beasts. He offered nothing she could wield as a weapon, polishing every lie until it shone like absolute candor. He preserved the ambiguities in his own clauses, denying her any foothold to reclaim abandoned breadth. 

When she finally rose to leave, Aldric glanced toward the corner.

The paladin and the pact-mage locked eyes across three tables and a bowl of cooling stew. Aldric offered a small, exact nod of civility. Banki returned the gesture, mirroring the precise angle of respect. 

Maren glanced over her shoulder, catching the silent exchange, fully aware that both men were actively diminishing the weight of the moment. 

The heroes walked out into the damp street. Banki finished his stew, recognizing the exact shape of the looming war. 

## What Maren Heard

Banki remained at the corner table, wiping the last of the broth with a crust of dark bread. He did not follow them into the fog. Instead, he let his physical body relax into the chair while his consciousness snapped along the Sovereign Thread, expanding outward to blanket the street and the tavern’s exterior walls. Strict, suffocating control. He pushed his awareness into the shadow-fae construct perched on the dripping eaves above the alley, borrowing its faceted vision to watch Maren Goldstring. 

She did not go far. She slouched near the alley mouth, out of the direct lamplight, adjusting her lute strap to cast herself as a weary traveler rather than a spy. She maintained an expression of profound boredom. Directed interest carried a physical weight, and paranoid men always felt it settle upon them. 

Banki smiled, a cold, isolated expression hidden in the tavern’s gloom. She was good. But she was playing against a rigged board. 

Through the shadow-fae’s ears, Banki listened as two dock workers stepped into the alley to share a smoke. He had hand-picked them that morning. He had written the script, memorized the cadence of their natural speech, and fed them the exact phrases required to weaponize Maren’s own history against her. 

"...the salvage man possesses something massive underground," the older worker muttered, lighting a twist of cheap tobacco. "The new Sablehook foundations go three times deeper than any standard beast enclosure. My cousin poured the sub-level concrete. He swears the reinforced doors down there don't lead anywhere reasonable."

"Doors to what?" his companion asked, perfectly on cue. 

"He doesn't know. They dismissed the regular crew before the interior work commenced. A shadow crew took over—men with no guild marks, appearing at dawn and vanishing at dusk."

Banki watched Maren’s posture tighten. Her breathing shallowed out, tuning purely to the vibration of the man's voice. 

"It isn't the crew that terrified him," the older man whispered, the smoke curling around his face. "It's the noise. He went back after hours. Stood by the Sablehook fence in the dead of night. He heard something bleeding up from the deep earth. Not machinery. Something like... like a melody turned inside out. Notes flowing backward. He claimed the sound made his teeth throb in his skull."

"His teeth?"

"Teeth and bone. He suffered a blinding migraine for three days. The worst part? He doesn't remember walking home. One moment he's standing by the fence, listening to the inverted song, and the next he's waking up in his bed with three hours completely wiped from his mind."

A deep, glacial dread bloomed in Maren’s mind, radiating so intensely that Banki tasted it across the telepathic link. Memory loss. Inverted sonic harmonics. Subterranean vaults. He watched her construct the exact nightmare he had designed for her. She was connecting the false rumors to the Choir, the psychic threat Aldric had supposedly shattered. 

She believed someone was employing the exact same psychic tools—memory manipulation and sonic pressure—hidden beneath the noise of a salvage yard. She believed Banki, the salvage king with the unsettling grey-green eyes and the perfectly curated facility tour, was rebuilding the enemy's machine. 

A kitchen porter stepped out the tavern’s back door to dump a bucket of slops, shattering her quiet revelation. The young man, his sleeves dusted with flour, loudly defended the Sablehook yard to the older workers. He insisted the salvage king paid his wages on time and provided sleeping quarters with thick interior locks. He spoke with the fierce, defensive pride of a man desperate to believe his newfound safety was real. 

"An interior lock doesn't make a house clean," the older dock worker grunted, playing his part to perfection. 

"It makes it safe," the porter fired back. "It's the first lock I’ve ever owned." When the older man mocked him for selling his soul to the salvage king, the porter tapped a folded paper in his breast pocket. "The contract claims my labor, not my soul. I read it twice."

Banki absorbed the phrase, recognizing the perfect, accidental resonance with his own Asmodean negotiations. *Title vests fully and exclusively in Banki. No recall. No reversion.* The contract claimed the labor. It did not claim the soul. 

Maren’s fingers tightened around her lute case. Banki read her frustration, the sharp agony of moral complexity. A clean narrative of absolute evil was easy to confront. Instead, she faced a tyrant who commanded fierce gratitude from the broken people he employed. Any warning she offered the boy would sound like the cruelty of a privileged stranger trying to tear down his only shelter. 

Maren turned and walked out into the dense, brine-scented fog. The city tasted of deception. She refused to run. Running attracted the wrong kind of eyes. She paused beneath a flickering streetlamp, checked her back trail in a dark windowpane, and vanished into the mist to find Aldric. 

Banki withdrew his consciousness from the shadow-fae, the faceted vision collapsing back into the warm, smoky reality of the tavern. The heroes were hunting a ghost he had painted on the wall. They would waste weeks preparing for a psychic onslaught, completely blind to the forty-two armored war-beasts currently dreaming beneath the breakfast hall. 

He stood, tossed a silver coin onto the scarred table, and walked out to secure the rest of the city. 

## The Outer Carry Expansion

The creature alley smelled of ammonia, wet fur, ozone, and profound desperation. The market operated in the permanent gloom beneath the upper trade tiers, a chaotic sprawl of iron cages, glass terrariums, and heavy canvas tents. Handlers shouted over the shrieks of exotic stock. Brokers dealt in flesh, bone, and living tools. 

Banki moved through the crowd with Kestrel perched silently on a high rafter above, her telepathic overwatch feeding him a constant stream of threats and opportunities. He carried himself with the heavy, unhurried grace of a sovereign. He had come to build infrastructure. 

He paused before a wide, reinforced cage holding a low-slung, heavily muscled creature covered in pale, dust-colored fur. Silt. The warning beast. 

The handler tried to sell her as a brute courier, boasting of her jaw strength and stamina. Banki ignored the man entirely, sinking his awareness into the beast’s erratic movements. She fed on multiple kills, yet her value lay entirely elsewhere. Silt was not a dramatic alarm beast; she was a subtle early-warning specialist. 

Banki watched her pattern. A sudden whisker tension and directional flare indicated air and seam discomfort. A paw-scrape against the iron bars betrayed structural unease. She exhibited repeated repositioning, avoiding stale pockets of air. She remembered places that felt wrong, paths that closed badly, and shafts she disliked for physical reasons. 

She was a living tunnel diagnostic instrument. A smart handler could learn to read her before danger became obvious. She would test routes before he sent better stock or people, marking dangerous or air-poor passages. 

He bought her outright, transferring her immediately to a trusted Shadow for stable housing and documented signal work. 

He moved deeper into the market, stopping at a small, impeccably clean stall lined with glass jars, rendered fats, and mineral stocks. Widow Sarn stood behind the counter, a social selector and vulnerability mapper who had repurposed her sharp eyes for industry. 

"I want a contractor," Banki stated, bypassing the usual market haggling. He projected his practical authority, letting the heavy, Shadow-Tainted presence of his pact bleed into the air. "You will have a dedicated workshop area in Serik’s Yard, and you will also create for the Crown’s supply. We will pay for the infrastructure, but you will need to take at least three apprentices."

The surrounding alley heard only fragments, but Widow Sarn heard the whole future hit her in the chest. She did not react like a fool offered glory. She reacted like a worker suddenly shown the exact amount of labor required to deserve a miracle. 

She set both hands flat on her shelf and thought for one breath. Two at most. 

"I’ll do it," she said. No tremble. No bargaining first. 

"Conditions?" Banki asked, respecting her absolute professionalism. 

"I want clean jars. A real render station. Dry storage. Mineral stock not cut with rubbish. And if you want three apprentices, I choose which three don’t poison my work by breathing on it wrong." She shot a hard look toward the gambling dens at the edge of the lane. "And I don’t train gamblers."

"Accepted," Banki said. "I will add the workshop allocation, dry storage, render station, and apprentice intake to the yard refit plan tonight."

Widow Sarn looked at him deeply, taking in the cold efficiency of the exchange. "You do move fast."

"That’s one of the safer things about us," Banki replied. 

He turned away, his attention snapping toward a commotion deeper in the broker-shadow lanes. Chainmoss Vell, a notoriously slippery transporter, stood beside a heavy iron-bound wagon. His fake handlers milled around, trying to look busy, but Kestrel’s telepathic ping hit Banki’s mind like a physical blow. 

*Sapient cargo. Hidden in the crate beneath the stone-echo shrike.* 

Banki closed the distance in five long strides. The Shadows materialized from the crowd, moving with terrifying synchronization. Before Vell could shout an order, his assistants were pinned against the wagon, their weapons stripped. 

Banki stepped up to Chainmoss Vell, grabbing the man by the collar of his heavy coat and slamming him against the iron bars of the shrike’s cage. The massive bird shrieked, echoing the impact. 

The alley went sharply quiet. The fake handlers froze. The real handlers listened. The broker ears swiveled, memorizing the sudden violence. 

Banki turned just enough that the whole creature alley could hear the ruling. His voice carried like a blade dragged slowly across stone. 

"Hidden persons in creature trade geometry are not cargo," Banki declared, the Sovereign Thread flaring with absolute command. "Not now. Not later. Not under euphemism. Not under clever crate work."

He let the silence stretch, forcing every trader in the market to feel the weight of the new law. 

"If you hide a sapient in stock transport," Banki continued, his voice dropping to a lethal whisper that somehow reached the back of the alley, "I will take the person, take the stock, take the gear, and take the rest of your day apart until I know whether I’m looking at rescue, transport, leverage, or trafficking."

The threat landed perfectly. No one in the alley mistook it for rhetoric. The morally rotten began silently revising their methods or their routes. 

Chainmoss Vell tried to breathe around the iron grip on his throat and failed. 

"Take him," Banki ordered. 

The Shadows hauled Vell and his crew away. Another team cracked open the false bottom of the crate, pulling a terrified, underdark-born youth into the safety of Jeren’s protection. The stone-echo shrike, the wagon, and the gear were seized. 

Banki stood in the center of the sudden void, letting the market take its fresh read of him. He had contracted a serious supplier into infrastructure, and he had publicly defined and enforced a hard line on hidden sapients. 

He was not merely a buyer, a sovereign, or a creature eccentric. The alley smelled the sudden, terrifying mixture of profit, standards, danger, and opportunity that might actually survive contact with the law. He was a market-maker. A standards-setter. A man who would build a supply line and kill an entire category of illicit trade in the exact same hour. 

Kestrel’s telekinetic cradle adjusted the newly purchased stock without a wasted gesture. Widow Sarn was already mentally organizing bulk jars and apprentice discipline. The lower channels of the city were permanently altered. 

Banki adjusted his cuffs, his mind already calculating the next strike against the Velmaren trafficking ring, and walked out of the alley with the city firmly in his grip.


Standard magical sight revealed nothing but brick and mortar. Banki peeled back that superficial layer, pushing his awareness deeper into the aether. The dampening field parted beneath his probing mind, exposing the suppressed currents beneath. Six distinct magical signatures pulsed within the building. Three belonged to living beings cloaked in active enchantments, while the remaining three clung to structural elements in a deliberate configuration. The lingering taste of old magic coated the walls, painting a clear picture of a workshop attached to a clandestine consultation room. Buyers frequented this space to negotiate prices for the bespoke, illegal crafting the Quiet Loft specialized in.

Tonight, he intended to breach it.

He took the vertical approach, shrinking his bones and flesh into his smallest, winged shape and wrapping himself in shadows. He scaled the building's exterior wall, his clawed fingers finding purchase on slick stone. The climb to the third floor took less than a minute. He pressed his small body against the sill of a window that projected an illusion of an empty room. Beneath the false image, two minds flickered with life alongside three active enchantments and the fading psychic echoes of a dozen recent clients.

A sophisticated ward laced the glass, shimmering like spun sugar in his expanded awareness. Banki lingered on the cold stone sill for three full minutes, mapping the enchantment's trigger conditions and secondary fail-safes. He approached the magic with the patient, meticulous attention of a master locksmith examining a mechanism he intended to defeat without ever touching the pins. The barrier was an elegant piece of paranoia. It would immediately alert the occupants the moment any physical substance breached the threshold—be it a sudden displacement of air, an unauthorized shift in ambient light, or the faint pressure of a lingering footstep—unless accompanied by a keyed authorization token. 

The enchantment was calibrated strictly for physical intrusion. It offered zero resistance to the mind.

A tiny, sharp smile curved Banki's altered face. He slipped his consciousness through the glass, letting it slide into the room like a knife through dark water.

The two minds inside blossomed in his awareness. 

The first belonged to a middle-aged female illusionist. Her consciousness fractured into a continuous, exhausting stream of maintenance, constantly adjusting a glamour here and reinforcing an illusion there. She kept the Quiet Loft's false face intact through a low-level, permanent exertion she had maintained for so long she no longer recognized the strain.

The second mind belonged to a client. His surface thoughts dripped with the entitled impatience of a wealthy man accustomed to buying time itself. He fumed over a delayed commission, pacing a mental cage built of irritation and arrogance. Banki sifted through the man's memories, extracting the name Verant Chalice. To the upper ward, Chalice played the respectable merchant. In the shadows, he trafficked in forged identification tokens, the kind that bypassed warded checkpoints. His current order consisted of four such tokens, each attuned to a different Harbour Magistracy official.

Banki filed the details away with the satisfaction of a collector. Verant Chalice was a piece on the board, securely positioned for future use. 

The illusionist proved more intriguing. Solenne, the operator of the Quiet Loft, represented the last independent remnant of a sprawling criminal crafting ring. Her mind hummed with a frantic, caged terror.

She knew extinction was coming. 

She anticipated his arrival, though she expected a violent raid. Banki lingered in her mind for another ten minutes, mapping the deeper structures of her memory to expose the full scope of her capabilities. 

He retreated his consciousness from the glass, leaving the physical space entirely untouched. 

Tomorrow, he would extend a formal offer. He would send the message through Sable, utilizing an intermediary in a carefully staged approach. The offer would combine carrot and stick in exact proportions, meticulously calibrated to exploit Solenne's frayed psychology. The carrot offered undeniable appeal: absolute protection, boundless resources, and a secure position within his growing subterranean infrastructure. The stick required no threats, only the quiet, demonstrated evidence that every other node in the criminal crafting ring had already been absorbed. The implicit suggestion hung heavy in the air: peaceful absorption was infinitely preferable to the violent alternative. 

The Quiet Loft would belong to him by the end of the week. 

Three nodes. Three acquisitions. In the thick fog, the respectable window continued to project its empty room.

## The Canal Walk

She met him on the canal walk at dusk, during the brief, suspended hour between the day's operations and the evening's obligations.

Sable had spent three days acutely aware of the warmth growing between them, refusing to give it a name lest someone weaponize it against her. She selected the canal walk because it belonged to neither of them. The space offered no desks, no ledgers, and no command boards. It provided only wet stone, low fog, and dark water carrying the city's refuse away.

Banki waited by the iron rail, blending into the gloom as if he were part of the architecture.

She approached from the east, holding her pace steady, forcing herself to walk without scanning the rooftops for snipers.

"You're a hard man to find," she called out.

Banki turned, his stillness unbroken. "Only for people who are looking in the wrong places."

She knew how she must look to him tonight. Her posture remained precise and controlled, but the control had fundamentally shifted—she was a coiled spring at rest, rather than one locked under punishing compression. She met his gaze, carrying the same dark, intelligent intensity she had wielded on the Greybridge Road, back when she was just a cloaked traveler counting exits and he was an invading stranger speaking directly into her mind. 

"I want to talk about something that isn't operational," she said.

"Yes."

"Are you reading my mind right now?"

"Yes," he replied. "I'm always reading. You know that."

A heavy pause stretched over the dark water. A spike of anger flared in her chest, immediately tangled with a dark, undeniable fascination. She swallowed the sudden surge of attraction, burying it beneath a layer of cultivated indifference, but she knew the effort only made the emotion more stark. She wanted in. She did not want into his bed first—though raw desire had just crossed the canal between them with the lethal speed of a thrown knife—she wanted into the terrifying power he was building. She craved the unique danger of standing as an equal beside it.

"I know what you want," Banki said. "And I know that you're capable enough to be useful and smart enough to be dangerous. Those are the two qualities I look for in every person I work with."

"And which quality do you value more?"

"Dangerous, without hesitation. Useful people are common. Dangerous people are rare. The most valuable employees in any organization are the ones whose employer is never entirely sure whether they're an asset or a threat."

Sable allowed her lips to curve in a concession rather than a smile. He had defined their dynamic perfectly, stripping away the diplomatic ambiguity that most partnerships relied upon.

"If this is going to be what I think it's going to be, I have conditions."

"You always have conditions. State them."

"When we are together—not working, not operating, not being the shadow network—you do not read my mind. Not my surface thoughts, not my deep thoughts, not the thoughts I have about you when I think you aren't looking. In those moments, I am not your operative. I am a person. That person requires privacy, and the privacy is not negotiable."

Banki's dark eyes narrowed. To a man who built his empire on absolute certainty, her demand to lock the most intimate door in her mind amounted to throwing away his strongest key. He relied on knowing every variable. Choosing her on these terms meant choosing a terrifying blindness, and she knew he despised how closely that surrender resembled actual trust.

"Accepted," he said slowly. "With one amendment."

"Which is?"

"In situations where lives are at immediate risk—yours, mine, or those of the people we are bound by blood-oath to protect—I reserve the right to read your surface thoughts without prior consent. I will not do this to protect you like a helpless child, nor to pull you from a necessary fight. I will only anchor your mind with my own, providing an invisible safety net so you can fight without restraint. The divine mathematics of our arrangement require you to remain an autonomous peer. The duration of my intrusion will not exceed the resolution of the immediate threat."

Sable weighed the amendment, turning it over in her mind. It chafed, but she understood the difference between intimacy and foolishness. 

"Accepted," she said.

A profound, sudden silence bloomed in her mind as Banki withdrew his presence. The exact moment he severed the connection struck her like a physical blow, pulling back the part of his consciousness that had rested on her by sheer habit. He relinquished his constant monitoring of her emotional weather and her fleeting intentions—the little thefts of certainty that made his chaotic existence tolerable. 

She vanished from his mental map, effectively blinding him. 

She remained standing before him in the physical world, keeping her hands perfectly still and her body balanced, the faint line of a scar visible just beneath the edge of her collar. But she had thrown the inner bolts. For the first time since their encounter on the Greybridge Road, he possessed no insight into her thoughts. 

The sudden emptiness in her own head brought a dizzying wave of vertigo, a shocking isolation after months of shared consciousness. 

"What am I thinking?" she challenged.

"I don't know."

Her expression remained perfectly neutral. "Say it again."

"I don't know."

The admission clearly cost him. Sable stepped closer, closing the distance until the heat of his body radiated against her skin. "That is the point."

"Blindness is an inefficient foundation for intimacy," Banki murmured.

"No," she countered. "Certainty is an efficient foundation for ownership. Intimacy starts where certainty stops."

Banki absorbed the words, his jaw tightening slightly as he recognized the irritating truth in her statement. She reached out and took his hand then, initiating contact purely as a woman making a choice, keeping the rest of herself walled off.

He held perfectly still, enduring the touch with an effort that radiated from his rigid posture. It was clearly the hardest thing he had done all day.

"Now," Sable said, "we can walk."

When she finally released his hand, the agreement stood firm. 

They walked until the fog swallowed the canal on both sides.

Sable bypassed Sablehook afterward. She navigated through three winding alleys, scrambled over a cooper's roof, descended the back stairs of a shuttered lace shop, and emerged into a lane smelling of rainwater and old ash. She maintained a strict discipline, checking her tail and clearing every corner, refusing to let her new emotional vulnerability compromise her survival instincts.

The house stood beyond the city's thickest wards, out where the warehouses thinned into salt grass and low stone walls. The cottage sagged under a rotting roof, its back garden overrun with nettles. A loose shutter on the east window knocked twice in the sea breeze. 

Sable stood before the door, the rusted key heavy in her palm, deliberately pushing Banki from her thoughts. She focused instead on the memory of a woman singing a low melody while kneading bread dough, and a man's strong hands lifting her up to mark her growing height on the wooden pantry frame. She remembered the soothing drum of rain on the thatch roof, and a shattered blue cup she had carefully hidden beneath the hearth stones. As a child, she had fervently believed that broken things could be saved simply by hiding them where the heat lived. She stood at the threshold of the very first place she had ever belonged, remembering a time long before belonging became a fatal liability.

She went inside and set to work repairing the shutter. She drove wooden pegs and wedged a new leather strip, angling the nails to accommodate the warped frame. Old houses required crooked solutions. When the wood finally held fast, the silence in the room shifted from abandoned to expectant. 

In the kitchen, beneath a loose floorboard, she found the letter wrapped in oilcloth. 

The handwriting addressed a name she had discarded years ago. Inside, her cousin had penned three pages of apologies and family news, ending with a line that ached with mundane hope: *Come home if there is still a home in you.*

Sable traced the words once, then struck a match against the stove. She burned the paper to ash, saving only the torn corner bearing her old name. She kept it as evidence, a tether to the ghost she used to be.

At dawn, she met the young girl known as S3 in the overgrown yard. 

The girl stood with her back against the crumbling stone, her weight balanced for flight. She held a knife exactly where a terrified child believed a knife belonged—visible enough to threaten, but too obvious to win a real fight. The stance grated on Sable's nerves, mirroring her own desperate posture at thirteen.

"If you show the blade, you ask the world to solve the blade," Sable instructed, her voice flat. "If you hide it, you force the enemy to solve you. Which problem do you want them working on?"

The girl shifted, sliding the weapon out of sight.

"Better. Again."

They drilled for an hour. Sable focused purely on evasion and survival, teaching the girl to identify exits, evaluate footwear, and memorize the swing of every door. She demonstrated how a rain barrel could conceal a body and explained the crucial difference between silence and true stillness.

"Does the fear ever leave?" S3 asked near the end of the session, wiping sweat from her brow.

"No. It just learns manners."

When Sable returned to Blackwater that evening, she crouched on a rooftop overlooking Sablehook and observed the compound. The fortified walls offered refuge, yet they also resembled a prison. Banki was constructing a sanctuary for the hunted while simultaneously building an engine for his own will. He would defend his people with terrifying ferocity, but he would also lock the gates from the inside.

She possessed the freedom to leave. Instead, she observed the kitchen lamps flicker to life and accepted the bitter reality that affection was much easier to survive when it anchored itself to someone less dangerous.

She descended into the compound and returned to work.

## Open Court

Mara had learned to love rooms that underestimated paper.

The Hall of the Crown's Justice operated differently. The architects of the Hall understood paper perfectly, constructing a sprawling legal labyrinth designed to exhaust the truth. In this chamber, advocates transformed murder into a misunderstanding and stretched a simple testimony across an entire week.

Mara strode into the echoing room carrying three ledgers, two stamped bonds, and one emergency writ. She had exhausted her patience for noblemen who wielded legality as a bludgeon.

Banki's presence pressed down from the observation gallery, a heavy, expectant weight against the back of her neck that she recognized without needing to turn around. Sable stood in the shadows near him, a silent guardian. At the defense table, Tally managed the extensive public books, his ink-stained fingers resting on the ledgers. Deep beneath the city streets, Sable's private extraction teams navigated the subterranean tunnels, coordinating their strikes perfectly while the public court systematically dismantled the aristocratic houses that had funded the Choir's atrocities.

House Velmaren crumbled first. 

The presentation of evidence lacked any theatrical flair, largely because the brutal truth rarely possessed the manners to arrange itself for a stage. It arrived in the form of tedious commercial receipts, banking transfer marks, grim custody lists, and meticulous cellar diagrams. It came through bulk medical purchases and the shaking, unbroken testimony of survivors. Together, the mundane documents painted a sterile, undeniable picture of kidnapping, slavery, murder, and high espionage against the Crown.

Lord Velmaren failed to appear. Sable had anticipated his flight and sealed his escape tunnel hours before the proceedings began. Mara let the opposing advocate object to the absence for exactly forty-seven seconds before slamming the interception record onto the polished oak table.

"My lord may answer in person when he finishes attempting to escape jurisdiction," she announced.

A sharp bark of laughter echoed through the chamber, quickly stifled as the gallery remembered the victims. 

House Rennic shattered next, followed by Hadrake, Merrow, Orseth, Pellor, and Veys. Mara did not destroy them; Banki preferred to acquire useful assets. She seized their accounts, restructured their holdings, and bound them under the very laws they had once purchased.

Then Velmaren's surviving advocate shifted the battlefield. He rose, brandishing a second writ and a smile polished by generations of entitlement. 

"Before this court accepts further accusations, we ask the Crown to address a matter of immediate civic danger," the advocate declared, his voice ringing against the stone. "Does Sablehook intend to maintain unregistered war-beasts in a civilian district under the fiction of household protection?"

The accusation swept through the chamber like oil across a spark.

A sudden, oppressive pressure swelled in the air, signaling Banki gathering his power in the gallery above—a cold surge meant to intimidate the room into silence. But magic could not save them here. If Banki threatened the court, Sablehook would become a besieged fortress. If he denied the creatures' existence, the inevitable discovery would frame them as a threat to every Crown Citizen. The city could authorize the slaughter of their stock and declare their sanctuary an armed encampment.

The combat-bred beasts were already purged from every public ledger. The inner circle guarded that devastating secret behind blood-sealed oaths and heavy magical wards. The Fourth Division, in the eyes of this courtroom, managed only the eastern caravans, standard rescue-haulage, and the mundane containment of ordinary beasts. It represented only the creatures Sablehook could publicly admit to owning without handing their political enemies a map directly to their throat.

Mara stepped forward before Banki could unleash his anger in a room built to punish it.

"Sablehook claims no private army," she stated, her voice cutting through the rising murmurs.

The advocate's smile widened.

"It claims public-facing Crown rescue and haulage assets under emergency household charter, district disaster practice, and voluntary civic damage bond."

The arrogant smile faltered, a victory Mara savored. 

She laid the first heavy bond on the witness table. It was properly stamped, witnessed, and bound in dull bureaucratic language that half the chamber immediately underestimated, while the other half went stark pale.

"If a Sablehook asset breaks a bridge, Sablehook repairs the bridge before any auction profit is distributed," Mara read aloud, her tone relentless. "If a handler injures a citizen, Sablehook pays the medical costs before any discretionary security expenditure. If Crown-protected people are harmed by Crown force, their claim receives priority hearing in our own books and public notice of payment."

"And who reviews the king?" the advocate sneered, recovering his composure.

Mara placed the second bond beside the first. "The people he owes."

Then she announced the financial figure.

A soft, collective gasp rustled through the wealthy attendees as they realized the paperwork had teeth. The amount was astronomical, backed by Sablehook's accounts, the seized Choir assets, and three revenue streams Banki had intended to keep entirely off the books. Spoken in Open Court, the bond became unbreakable law. It made the beast caravans answerable, prioritizing civilian repairs over profit, and transforming collateral damage from a private embarrassment into a public debt.

Mara had sacrificed one of Banki's strategic advantages to protect his people. 

The true cost lay in reputation and expectation. The hidden kingdom now bore a public promise, and enemies armed with ink could make that promise excruciatingly expensive.

Up in the gallery, Banki went dangerously still. The pressure in the air vanished, replaced by an icy void.

*Good,* Mara thought. *Feel it.*

"This bond also reinforces what Crown Citizenship already guarantees," she continued, driving the final nail. "No loyal citizen may be silenced, memory-altered, or contractually threatened for reporting harm caused by Crown force. No child under Crown protection may be used as leverage in civil defense. No protected worker may be turned into a shield and then called grateful."

The chamber stared at the Sablehook delegation with new eyes. To them, Sablehook was no longer a rogue threat; it was an accountable institution.

"You expect this court to trust monsters because you have made them expensive?" the advocate demanded, his voice cracking slightly.

Mara met his gaze, letting the exhaustion of endless nights spent arranging witness protection, custody clauses, and handler exits show plainly on her face. 

"No. I expect this city to remember damage with names, prices, and witnesses."

A heavy silence reigned. Finally, the presiding authority struck his gavel, accepting the bond into the public record. 

Mara refused to look up at the gallery. She knew the shape of Banki's anger, and she recognized the grudging respect buried beneath it. He could have commanded the room's terror, but he could never have secured its legal permission.

Three minutes later, a runner slipped a slate onto her table. 

*Acceptable,* Sable had written in sharp chalk strokes. *Also, never use the phrase "military animals" in a public document. The inner circle holds.*

Mara allowed herself a single, shaky exhale. She turned back to the evidence table and resumed taking the noble houses apart, piece by piece. 

When a recess was called, she stepped into the echoing corridor, pressed one hand flat against the cold stone wall, and let the tremors rack her body for exactly three seconds. Then she smoothed her robes and returned to the battlefield before anyone could use her exhaustion against her.

Outside the Hall, the city learned what Sablehook would pay when protection broke something. Under Sablehook, the Fourth Division continued teaching the beast caravans to carry weight without becoming war. Deeper than that, behind a seal the court would never survive uncovering, the inner circle kept the combat tether breathing.

A kingdom required bread, contracts, roadways, grave records, forge priorities, and the terrible question of what force was allowed to do in the name of care.

For the first time, Banki's answer possessed witnesses he could not fully control.

The Belowmarket was waiting.

## The Red Shelves

Dawn broke over the forward camp without an ounce of warmth, bringing a grey, hard light to the space between the Brinewood and Shattercliff. The fires smoked beneath blackened kettles, and the camp reeked of damp leather, rhino musk, axle grease, and caged beasts.

Banki stood by the map crate, a mug of bitter coffee in his gloved hand. He wore traveling leathers over hidden plates, with throwing knives tucked into easily accessible sheaths. Kestrel crested the misty slope, her reptilian bulk moving with liquid grace. Rinna and Bram flanked her, the two human scouts carrying the morning's labor on their clothes. Mud coated their boots to the calf, topped with a thick layer of chalky dust, and sharp brambles had snagged their cloaks. Kestrel bore the landscape differently—shale dust coated the sharp curves of her foreclaws, a massive black thorn pierced her leather harness seam, and condensation beaded heavily on the glass lenses of the far-seeing charm secured at her throat. She held her heavy tail low, a deliberate posture designed to prevent her body language from speaking before she did. Rinna's heavy hood carried the scent of damp stone and crushed juniper, while Bram remained entirely oblivious to a thick strip of green lichen clinging to his left shoulder.

"The ridges open in two miles," Kestrel reported, her speaking charm translating her clicks and hisses into a rough, exact human dialect. "After that, the country breaks into dry cuts and sharp drops. We found an old wash where something massive has been feeding."

"How massive?" Banki asked.

"It left heavy drag marks and shattered shell shards," Rinna offered, her voice scraping like a whetstone on steel. "No dragon or rhino feeds that way. It moved with a bear's cunning but lacked its shape."

"That narrows the field," Banki noted.

Bram scrubbed dirt from his eye. "We also found signs on the west edge. Two, maybe three scouts. They got close enough to count our wagons, then lost their nerve when the perimeter shifted."

"They were measuring our nerve, not losing theirs," Vesper corrected. She emerged from the tree line at the opposite angle, her recurve bow unstrung but her grip never truly at rest. "There is a distinct difference. I tracked one to a hollowed deadfall. He maintained excellent breath discipline and used cheap boot grease, but he had a fresh ink stain on his right cuff. They weren't simple road thieves."

Banki took a slow sip of the bitter coffee and let his ever-present mental lattice expand. He ran his awareness thin through the bustling camp and outward into the dense treeline, tasting the emotional residue, the lingering intentions, and the stale ghost-rime of thoughts already departed. Minds left distinct, undeniable marks when they strayed close enough, especially frightened minds. The scouts were wrong about the nature of the fear. Pure terror left a greasy, metallic tang on the air. Cold calculation possessed sharp, geometric edges. Elaborate lies left a cloying sweetness that curdled the instant a telepath bit into it. 

"There were three watchers," Banki confirmed at last, reading the fading echoes. "One hid behind the deadfall. One crouched by the washstone with the chipped white vein. The third hung back entirely out of bowshot, radiating a bitter, familiar self-loathing."

Bram blinked in surprise. "You can pull all that from the dirt?"

"I can pull enough."

Kestrel shifted her weight, her tail sweeping the ground. "The third watcher favored his left knee and carried oiled steel. He counted our handlers instead of our wagons. He reports to a patient employer who pays for numbers, not heroics."

Banki inclined his head, acknowledging the drake's tactical assessment. He had altered and armed Kestrel's mind, elevating her beyond a mere beast of burden. She understood hierarchy, profit, and the psychological impact of a predator that could count.

He set his mug down on the crate. "We proceed in phases. First, secure the route. Second, harvest the beasts. Third, manage the reputation. Tally holds the paperwork. Gravewire manages the perimeter. Kessa keeps the cages locked. Vesper, take the watchers alive if they present the opportunity. Cleanly."

"And the memories afterward?" Vesper asked.

"Standard doctrine. Wipe the incident. Leave them with vague impressions of thirty wagons, predator tracks, and generalized fear. Elaborate lies always collapse under their own weight."

The meeting dissolved into motion. Handlers strapped armor onto the rhinos, checked the cage pins, and settled into the practiced rhythm of dangerous labor. 

Banki took the vanguard as the road fractured into jagged stone shelves. The terrain hardened, the soil thinning to expose glinting mineral veins in the rock. 

Midmorning brought them to the kill-site Rinna had described. The basin lay littered with white shell fragments, some crushed by raw impact, others peeled open by a patient, terrifying jaw. Dark blood stained a leaning slab of shale, accompanied by a pool of iridescent spit that shimmered with a sickly rainbow hue.

Rinna crouched by the stain. "A feeder. Mature."

"Does it climb?" Hark asked, tightening his grip on his spear.

"Everything worth fearing climbs."

The warning proved immediately prophetic. 

The attack didn't come from the basin, but from the overhang above, concealed by thorn brush and grey ivy. One moment the rock face was empty; the next, it exploded downward in a shower of debris. The beast resembled a blunt, armored monitor lizard, its hide encrusted with thick mineral plates. A long, combed tongue whipped through the air, searching for flesh.

Hark shouted a warning just as Vesper dove to the side. 

Banki reached out with his mind, seizing the creature's equilibrium and violently twisting it. The beast slammed into the shale shoulder-first, losing its footing as Vesper hurled a weighted net over its snapping jaws. 

It was too massive to subdue with simple leverage. 

Banki drove his awareness into the feeder's furious mind, locating the pain receptors in its bruised shoulder and amplifying the sensation until the creature shrieked and convulsed. Vesper threw a second net, Hark secured a loop over its snout, and Gravewire rushed in with heavy chains. 

The beast surged, rippling with sudden, monstrous strength. Shale shattered beneath its claws. The translucent tongue lashed out, slicing across Hark's forearm and peeling the skin to the muscle in three bright, bloody ribbons. The creature rolled, dragging Hark and Vesper across the stones, and slammed Gravewire against the basin wall. The impact forced a sickening, breathless wet sound from the handler's chest.

Banki dropped the subtle manipulations and drew upon the deeper currents. He shaped raw, marrow-deep magic into a wailing curse of bone-dust and unleashed it. The blast struck the beast like a physical blow, ringing against its mineral plates and disrupting the flesh beneath. 

The feeder lurched, disoriented. Vesper drove an arrow into the soft joint of its hind leg. Kestrel plunged from the ridge above, reversing her heavy harness blades mid-air. She drove one deep into the precise seam where the thick mineral growth split away from the muscle behind the creature's jaw. Hot, black blood sprayed violently across her chest harness, stippling the pale stone with thick, coin-shaped drops. The horrific smell hit the air instantly—a sharp mineral reek that smelled of deep brine, hot copper, and struck flint.

"Now!" Banki ordered. 

Gravewire limped forward on a ruined knee, wrapping the chain around the forelimbs. Hark shoved a reinforced pole through the net to gag the jaws. Banki shifted his unseen grip from subtle disruption to overwhelming compression. He clamped down with an invisible manifestation of pure will, seizing the beast at four distinct anchor points simultaneously: the lower jaw, the left forelimb, the right hind limb, and the thick base of the tail. He applied a crushing, localized gravity, bowing the creature's spine under the impossible pressure. Bones despised being argued with from four different directions. Something deep inside the beast cracked loudly, followed immediately by another sickening snap. The feeder finally let out a defeated shriek, the sound resembling a sheet of slate splitting under the crushing weight of winter ice.

"Keep it alive," Kessa yelled over the din. 

"It is alive," Banki grunted, maintaining the pressure. 

"Barely!"

"Barely suffices."

They secured the beast with sweat, blood, and brute leverage. Once the feeder lay bound under heavy canvas, Kestrel wiped the black blood from her blades, and Kessa rushed to bind Hark's bleeding arm. 

Banki knelt beside the thrashing, trussed head. He sifted through the beast's frantic, pain-clouded thoughts. He tasted memories of warm rock nests, blind hatchlings, and the salty crunch of shelled prey. Deeper still, he found flashes of men snatched near an ancient staircase, and a searing memory of fire.

"Unique enough," Banki murmured.

Rinna looked up from her pack. "What did you find?"

"It remembers stone steps."

The handlers paused, exchanging wary glances. 

"Old ruins?" Bram asked.

"Older than you, but used by men in the last season," Banki replied, rising to his feet. "Mark this location on the map. We don't follow the trail today."

"Because it's a trap?" Vesper asked, slinging her bow.

"Because everything in this world is a trap," Banki said, turning back toward the wagons. "This particular one is just too expensive for today's budget."

The handlers lashed the feeder to a drag-frame, hauling it back to the caravan over the grinding complaints of stone and scale. Before noon, Vesper and Kestrel captured the scouts as well. Vesper caught the first man off-guard when a full bladder forced him from cover. The second scout proved more capable and very nearly escaped. He broke from the dense scrub at the edge of the drag-line and sprinted downhill, moving with the low, desperate speed of a man who realized he had waited exactly one breath too long. Kestrel flicked a weighted harness blade with casual precision. The spinning steel bit into the back of his knee, opening the tendon flawlessly without severing the leg. The scout went over in a violent roll, scrambling up with a desperate knife in his hand, only to find Banki already waiting inside his mind, ready to rewrite the entire morning.


Banki worked his fingers into the trembling architecture of the runner’s mind. The skull yielded in strata: first raw terror, then the crisp lines of embedded orders. Count the wagons. Count the handlers. Verify the Blackwater papers. Report back to a man with lamp soot under his fingernails and unnaturally clean teeth. The priority phrase hummed in the captive's memory: *Mirrorhouse sings.* 

Banki left the foundation intact. A stripped mind invited unwelcome questions from competent enemies. Instead, he laid new beams across the old structure, pressing his thumbs against the runner's temples until the man gasped. 

*Thirty wagons rolled past,* Banki etched into the soft clay of the man's recollection. *Predator tracks scarred the mud, broad as a gate. Shadows detached themselves from the tree line. The thing in the woods had no eyes, but it whispered your true name.*

The false memory rooted itself immediately. Terror offered excellent soil for ugliness. Banki withdrew his reach, his own pulse thudding with a dull ache behind his eyes—the toll of holding two realities at once. He signaled his outriders with a flick of his wrist. They would escort this watcher and the first one miles off the route and turn them loose in opposite directions. 

By afternoon, the road surrendered to the procession. They halted at a sour waystation built from sagging sheds and surrounded by cookfires. Tack menders and clerks mingled with enough listening strangers to make a manufactured rumor profitable.

Tally laid out the permits one by one, his callused fingers gentle on the vellum. 

The clerk traced the three-spoke wheel crest and the lower-spoke nick. The pressure of the seal held true. The dark ink bore no signs of forgery. The clerk's pulse, a steady thrum against Banki's awareness, hitched.

“It’s all in order,” the clerk murmured.

“Of course it is,” Tally replied.

Banki offered a deliberate, open smile to the room. “We are building something regular. Regular things live longer. Tell anyone with a legal appetite and sufficient means that Sablehook will be receiving verified interest.”

“Verified for what?” a shadow near the benches asked.

Banki let the silence stretch, giving the weight of his presence time to settle over the cookfires. “Specialty salvage. Regulated acquisitions. The sort of things that improve in value when fools are kept thirty feet away from them.”

He left the statement on the table, sharp and gleaming. By the time the wagons rumbled out again, the Sablehook name had already leaped ahead, carried in three different mouths with three distinct variations of the truth. 

Towards evening, the terrain jutted into redder, steeper angles. In a shallow cave behind weather-whitened thorn lay the ruin of another feeder. Something massive had opened the creature from throat to vent, consuming only the vital organs and leaving muscle, shell ridges, and the heavy skull. The blood still held a bruise-dark shine in the crevices. The kill was fresh. The predator possessed a discerning palate. 

Banki crouched beside the gore. The air grew thick, carrying the metallic tang of impending violence.

He dug into his pouch, retrieving a vial of human bone-dust. He crushed the fragile glass between his fingers and traced ancient marrow runes in the damp air. The magic exacted its price immediately, drawing warmth from his extremities until his fingers went numb. The Greater Shroud draped over him, cold and heavy. He lifted from the ground, drifting upward in total silence toward a black crevice in the cliff face.

Inside waited a beast born of violence and starvation. Its mind radiated pain, the frantic urge to escape, and the imprinted geometry of cages. 

When it sprang, it made no sound. The cave mouth flashed with claws and glass-thread whiskers. 

The creature recovered from its missed strike instantly, pivoting on the stone. Banki channeled the marrow runes, driving a stunning pulse of raw, concussive force through the air. The magic tore a gasp from his lungs, leaving a copper tang on his tongue as the beast folded into a twitching heap.

It snarled even in its stupor. 

“Good,” Banki whispered. Blood ran warm down the back of his hand from a gash he hadn't realized he'd taken. “I dislike merchandise that surrenders early.”

Night found them under the hard glitter of the Shattercliff stars. They possessed two new high-value problems, three extra canisters of harvested cliff-light dust, suspect papers, a captive runner, and a perimeter maintained by absolute competence. 

Banki leaned back against a wagon wheel, sorting through the phrases stolen from the runner's head. *Mirrorhouse. Nella Quist. Tidepin Steps. Hollow reed post. Twice a day when Mirrorhouse sings.* The city reached for them from miles away, and he welcomed the contact. Work always improved when it pushed back.

## The House With Teeth

By the time the wagons rumbled into Blackwater Quay, they had transformed from a hunting party into a traveling vault. They hauled captured feeders, cliff-light dust, and the weight of growing rumors. Scavengers and merchants tracked their progress from waystations to charcoal-burner camps. Banki met every inquisitive stare with calculated indifference, seeding the road with precise half-truths until the countryside echoed with the Sablehook name. 

At a muddy camp halfway to the swamp-low lanes, a woman with soot-blackened hands squinted at the convoy. “That the Sablehook lot? Word is they pay fair and keep their papers cleaner than bishops keep their bastards.”

Banki offered her a sliver of a smile. “We try to avoid the clergy’s habits, and we never charge extra.”

She laughed, a harsh, genuine sound that followed them into the dusk.

They dragged mud, blood, and their new acquisitions into the Sablehook yard long after nightfall. Sable waited with four full Shadows, two probationers, city backups, and a sharply focused intensity. The yard functioned around her as an extension of her will. A lifted finger opened the intake lane. A glance sent probationers scurrying to the outer walls. The Shadows kept their attention anchored on her, proving she had spent the intervening hours teaching them exactly where authority resided. 

“You took your time,” Sable murmured. 

“We brought things worth the minutes.”

Her gaze swept over the fresh cages, lingering on the canvas-draped feeder groaning against its chains. “That one?”

“Unique enough to require better adjectives before sale.”

“Good.”

There was no warmth, only the immaculate machinery of their partnership. Hooded lanterns swung in the dark. The outer gate barred shut. The intake lane swallowed the wagons. 

They labored into the deep hours. Banki inspected the water tank, the chained feeder, the glass-whisker capture, the glass jars, and every handler whose hands trembled. 

Darric arrived before dawn, his coat splattered with travel mud and his eyes bright with scholarly greed. 

“You paid me five hundred platinum to come by dawn,” Darric announced, stepping past the owner’s lane boundary. “I came before I could misbehave and spend it.”

“I respect initiative.”

Darric peered through the screened viewing line. His pupils blew wide. A breathless, almost religious awe softened his features. “You lunatic.”

“Professionally.”

Darric’s privileges came with strict boundaries: owner-lane only, no unsupervised viewing, and absolutely no notes leaving the premises without approval. Banki guided him through the inventory, sorting the stock by rank and risk. They catalogued the Named, the Unique, the Rare, and the Common. 

They paused before the chained glass-whisker beast. The creature paced in tight, frustrated circles. 

“This one,” Darric whispered, leaning dangerously close to the bars, “has broken three cages in its life.”

“Only if it survives to be sold badly,” Banki replied, gripping Darric’s shoulder and pulling him back an inch. 

Darric turned, his expression caught between horror and admiration. “You don’t collect creatures. You collect arguments. Each of these is an argument waiting for a buyer foolish enough to believe he understands it.”

“That is the most flattering thing anyone has said to me in days.”

They audited the inventory until the sheer volume exhausted Darric’s enthusiasm. Four hundred eighty jars lined the shelves—a kingdom in glass. Money, risk, and reputation stood physical and undeniable. Darric scribbled provisional categories on his slate, scratching them out seconds later in a fit of academic frustration. 

When the owner’s lane finally closed and Darric departed under escort, Sable sat at the main ledger table. Lysa Varn sat on her left, Hesta Bramblewake on her right. Sable had transformed the scarred wood into a nerve center. Intake slates piled on one side, payment books on the other. Conditional hires lay in the center, weighted down by an unsheathed dagger. 

A second slate rested half-hidden beneath the table, bearing notes on south canal rumors, missing children, and a margin note in Sable’s precise hand: *Conceal from Banki until exits are prepared.* The discrepancy burned itself into Banki's memory without altering his expression. 

Lysa, the oddities vendor, exuded the wary poise of someone accustomed to profits that bit back. 

Hesta lifted her chin from a stop-condition slate. “Your people keep wanting things to love them.”

“They keep wanting not to be killed,” Banki corrected, leaning over the table. 

“Same disease with better boots.” Hesta tapped her slate with a charcoal stub. “For breeding programs, you stop when the eyes change wrong, when the appetite accelerates, or when mothers cease responding to their young. And if handlers start claiming the beasts ‘know them,’ they need replacing. That tone is for men waiting on their own funerals.”

“Noted.”

“And if anyone suggests hand-rearing something whose mother can still bite, I want them slapped with a shovel.”

“Also noted.”

Lysa slid her contract forward. “Adjacent market placement, twenty-four-hour protection during onboarding, first right on brineglass oddities, and a stall once the outer courtyard exists.”

“It will exist,” Sable promised. 

“Then I will pretend that comforts me.”

Banki pressed his seal into the wax, folding another alliance into the house. Sablehook was rapidly outgrowing its origins as a holding cell. It boasted pens, doctrine, consultants, and frontage. It was a market with a throat full of teeth, and Blackwater Quay had plenty of room for such things to grow. 

***

Tovin Vale preferred the pantry. The rest of Sablehook reeked of damp leather, frightened animals, and the sweetish, cloying stench of marrow-dust magic that made his teeth ache whenever the handlers dragged their containment nets past the kitchens. But the pantry smelled only of flour and cold stone. 

He sat on a three-legged stool beside a massive flour bin, a basket of bent spoons wedged between his boots. 

In the Belowmarket, broken things became garbage. If a spade snapped, the overseers kicked it into the canal. If a boy coughed twice before the morning shift, they left him on the wharf for his lungs to sort themselves out. Branna’s kitchen, however, operated on different principles. 

Tovin lifted a silver-plated spoon. The bowl was pressed flat on one side, bearing a crescent-shaped dent from a heavy heel. He ran his thumb over the smooth curve where the base metal showed through in dull grey streaks. 

*Metal remembers force,* Kestrel had told him once, adjusting the leather on her training harness. *If you bend a buckle too fast, it snaps. Work it slow, and the steel stays strong. It remembers the pull.*

Useful things survived being bent. 

Tovin gripped his screwdriver—the one Tally had found for him, complete with a polished ash handle that fit his palm perfectly. He pressed the flat head against the crescent dent. Using his knee for leverage, he worked the wood slowly, coaxing the silver back into a bowl. His shoulder blade clicked with the effort. 

“A spoon is for stirring,” he whispered to the flour barrel. “If it still holds grease, it isn't ruined. It's just a different shape.”

The kitchen gate rattled in the distance. The breakfast hall remained empty, though the rhythmic, heavy tread of Darric’s rhino teams echoed from the north lane. Tally would be in the office, his pen scratching across parchment. 

Sablehook operated on rules. Tovin appreciated the predictability. On the streets, a man with a knife took your boots because no ledger forbade it. Here, Banki’s laws lived in neat columns. A child who ate the house biscuit belonged to the house. If a handler stared at him with the grey, hungry eyes common to Belowmarket sellers, Tovin only had to point at the doorpost to make them look away. 

He had tested the pantry latch six times that morning. The iron was clean and well-greased. He had even scratched a note under the bottom shelf: *PANTRY LATCH OPENS FROM INSIDE.* If the door ever shut and the handlers misplaced the key, any child trapped inside needed to know they could still escape. 

He examined the spoon. The dent had mostly vanished. The silver bore scars where the heel had bitten deep, but it would hold porridge. 

He tossed it into the pile on his right. 

A shadow pooled beneath the bottom shelf. 

Tovin held his breath. Shadows in the pantry usually flickered when the heat-stones cooled, but this one crept forward across the damp flagstones. It spread as a thin, oily smear of black water, carrying the scent of salt-sunk silt and old copper. 

The air plummeted in temperature. 

Tovin pressed his fingers against the pocket of his jerkin, seeking the half of Branna's morning biscuit he had saved. The crumb offered a faint, warm vibration against his thigh, carrying the warding sigils Banki had stitched into the flour. 

“I am here,” Tovin whispered, his voice trembling against the stone. “Tovin Vale. I am sorted.”

The shadow slid over the toe of his boot. 

He braced for the bite of a coal-whip or the cold pinch of iron filings. Instead, a wet, suffocating weight pressed into his skull. A thousand overlapping voices swelled in the dark, forming a chorus devoid of individuals. 

The spoon slipped from his grasp, striking the flagstones with a bright, silver click. 

He lunged for the pantry latch, but his hand hung limp. His fingers refused the iron. The muscles had simply forgotten how to make a fist. 

*The metal remembers,* the chorus sang through his own thoughts, hijacking his inner voice to build the strange, echoing notes. *It does not hurt if it still stirs.*

Tovin Vale swallowed his scream. He stared at the empty basket between his boots. Who would sort the rest of the spoons now that his screwdriver lay out of reach on the floor?

## The Belowmarket

The Black Stair descended into an engineered, institutional gloom that tasted of iron, salt, and stale fear. 

Banki walked down the slick steps with Mirell at his side. Jeren and FateShear secured the angles behind them, while Kestrel prowled the deeper darkness of the saltward flank, ensuring nothing escaped through the tunnels connecting the Belowmarket to the sea. 

The awareness of the space unfolded in distinct layers. Guards projected cold, procedural routines. They calculated exactly how far a chain could tighten before breathing became a liability. Operators and clerks buzzed with the efficient, bureaucratic indifference that made atrocities possible. 

Then, the crushing weight of the captives reached him. 

The sheer silence of the children nearly broke his concentration. They projected a profound structural waste. Growth had been stunted, muscles thinned at the wrists, and nervous systems dulled by chemical compliance. These were futures dismantled for inventory. 

They did not scream. They had retreated into the final, defensible corners of their own minds, curling into spaces so small that hope could no longer fit beside them. 

Banki paused on the final step, letting Cell Nine bleed into his thoughts. 

The memory surged: the observation window, the blinding restraint light, the bite-block. The cold silver scale that weighed a tiny body as if mass and value were identical concepts. Aureth Voss’s patient voice detailing preservation rules while Banki’s own hands strained against the straps. They had called him *specimen* because the word *child* would have indicted them. 

This was the Thessalan Consortium operating under a new city’s face. 

The children below had not chosen their cages. He knew the exact geometry of that helplessness in his marrow. He had burned the Consortium to the ground, and he had spent every year since ensuring he would never wear chains again. 

These cages would end. 

“Mirell,” Banki said, his voice stripped of all warmth. “Full suppression. Every guard. Every operator. Every conscious mind in this facility that is not a captive.”

Mirell’s tentacles unfurled, precise and perfectly silent. 

She released a keyed suppression wave, striking only the hostile minds. Every guard collapsed. Every clerk, administrator, and lookout crumpled to the stone, their brains short-circuiting under the targeted pressure. Twenty-three perpetrators fell into heavy, dreamless unconsciousness. 

The captives remained untouched. Mirell navigated their terror with a delicacy that highlighted the brutal efficiency she applied elsewhere. Cortisol, starvation, and obedience-drug residue flared in her wake; Banki moved through the gloom, opening iron doors and tearing away restraints before panic could take root. 

He worked with controlled urgency, summoning arcane gates in the stale air. 

He lifted the children who could not walk, guiding the rest through the shimmering portals. Twelve adults and eleven children passed through the thresholds. 

Among them was Wren. 

She was eight years old. When Banki swung her cage door wide, she scrambled backward, pressing herself against the damp wall. His human disguise offered no comfort; children recognized monsters by the pressure they carried, not the shape of their faces. A dark stain spread across her ragged dress as her bladder let go. Shame hit her so violently it briefly eclipsed her terror. 

Banki offered no hollow reassurances. He brushed the surface of her mind, plucking the necessary truths: Wren, mother alive, home still standing. He wove a single instruction into the air, pressing it gently toward her. 

“Through the light. The woman there is safer than I am.”

Wren stumbled through the gate. On the other side, Sable waited. She did not smile or attempt to soothe the girl. Instead, she unclasped her heavy cloak, wrapped it around Wren’s shivering shoulders, and placed herself squarely between the child and the portal. 

Banki closed the final gate. The stone chamber grew suffocatingly quiet. 

For one long breath, the lawful architecture of his mind held. The ledgers, the oaths, the carefully constructed routines of Sablehook—law provided a door to keep the rage locked away. 

Then an unconscious operator twitched on the floor. A sluggish thought rolled loose from the man’s mind: irritation over damaged merchandise. 

The locked door in Banki's mind blew off its hinges. 

The room was about to discover what he had kept contained for centuries. 

He started with hands. 

The first operator woke to Banki unfolding his fingers, one joint at a time. Banki separated the man’s choices from his instruments. He ruined the thumb that gripped the whip, then shattered the hand that wrote *child-height* beside *resale value*. Names spilled from the man’s lips before the second hand snapped. 

The second man had swallowed a charm-key. Banki tracked its arcane ticking beneath the operator’s sternum. He dragged a narrow, telekinetic line through the flesh, parting skin and muscle to lift the blood-slicked mechanism into the air. He turned the man’s terror inward, using it to pry open the memories attached to the key. 

Screams began to fill the vault. 

Down in the saltward dark, Kestrel sent a pulse of clean, predatory patience through their bond. The tunnels were sealed. Her claws scraped against the stone. 

Mirell spoke his name once. 

Banki registered the warning, but he did not stop. 

Varen Pell surrendered inventory routes and storage names the moment Banki stripped him of his secrets. Varen had prided himself on organizing the children by obedience response. Banki forced him to stare as his own memories spooled across the damp wall in wet, glowing sigils, written in blood drawn directly from Varen’s capillaries. When Varen squeezed his eyes shut, Banki adjusted the muscles in his neck, forcing them open. 

“Read,” Banki whispered. 

Varen read until his vocal cords tore. 

Sella Vane relied on her House Velmaren connections. Survival, however, did not equate to mercy. Banki peeled her protections away piece by piece: her patron, her lover, her blackmail chains. He ripped out the memory of a nursery she had walked past three times without stopping, making her understand that her usefulness was the only barrier between her and absolute ruin. 

Dhorik Vrenn carried a judge’s signet in his boot and a child’s milk tooth in his pouch for luck. Banki found the tooth. The screaming took on a ragged, desperate pitch. 

The lawful part of Banki catalogued the names, the buyers, the corrupt clerks, and the compromised priests. The rest of him let Cell Nine dictate the rhythm. He took the Belowmarket apart, person by person. Every broken bone asked a question; every severed nerve demanded an answer. One operator invoked his gods; Banki pinned the prayer to the wall and excised the tongue that formed it. Another bargained with hidden addresses. Banki drafted a contract in the man's own blood, demanding an address for each finger he wished to keep. When the man lied on the second address, Banki smiled—a sharp, cold expression—and collected the penalty before the truth poured out. 

When silence finally returned, the living operators shuddered in their bonds, utterly emptied. The dead served as gruesome warnings draped across the counting tables. Every ledger bore a bloody handprint that no clerk could ever file away. 

Banki stood in the center of the slaughter. The wet room stank of opened bodies and centuries of suppressed rage given form. 

He turned his back on the carnage, climbing the stairs until he emerged into daylight that tasted of freedom and the impending rain. 

The Belowmarket ceased to exist. 

***

The children found safety, but they did not find peace. Wren refused to close her eyes unless Sable’s heavy cloak rested within arm's reach. Two older captives rejected bread until Branna ate from the basket first. A boy dove beneath a bench whenever a door creaked. Rescue had not illuminated their world; it had merely pushed the dark back far enough to allow them to breathe. 

By afternoon, another strata of the city belonged to Banki. He claimed it not through gentleness, but through the undeniable math of survival: the rescued lived, the predators did not. 

Wren’s mother arrived at the Hall of the Crown’s Justice early the next morning. 

Banki did not attend. Instead, he anchored his consciousness in the mind of a young guard stationed outside the family reunification chamber. The guard projected a mix of professional stiffness and raw emotional exhaustion. 

The guard’s vision framed the small, warm room, deliberately stripped of institutional coldness. 

Wren sat wrapped in a blanket, tearing at a loaf of bread with the single-minded focus of a body that remembered starvation better than safety. 

The chamber door swung inward. 

The guard’s breath hitched. Wren looked up. Recognition flared in the girl's hollow eyes. She dropped the bread, the crust scattering across the floor, and bolted. 

Her mother caught her, sinking to her knees and wrapping her arms around the girl with a ferocity that defied the world to try and take her again. 

Wren clung to her mother's neck for three long breaths. Then, slowly, she looked over her mother’s shaking shoulder, staring out toward the open doorway. She wasn't seeking Banki, but the looming possibility of him. Her small fingers dug into the fabric of Sable’s cloak, gripping it until her knuckles turned bone-white.


"Is the shining man gone?" Wren whispered.

Her mother only stared, uncomprehending. Sable, rain still dripping from her sleeves by the wall, answered.

"Yes."

Wren buried her face in her mother's shoulder, sobbing.

Behind the guard's borrowed vision, the wet blur of the room swam. The guard's mind pulsed with a single, frantic rhythm, a raw plea rather than a thought: *thank the Sovereign thank the Sovereign thank the Sovereign*.

Banki severed the mental tether.

Back in the Sablehook secure room, Banki stared at his palms.
